| /api/api-keys (CRUD) | openapi (api-keys) | done | `apikeys.tov` (routes, the value shown once, views, paging, validation); `ops.tov` `apiKeysRoute` and `heldOf` (the ceiling: a caller grants only permissions it holds; the admin and the legacy credential hold all, a custom role its pairs, a key its own); `rbac.tov` `keyOf`, `keyAllows` and `authorize` (a key is checked by its permissions alone and never inherits its creator's role; an expired key is a 401); `store.tov` `setApiKeys` (governance.api_keys keeps a SHA-256 of each value). The APIKeys resource (View, Create, Update, Delete) is appended to the RBAC catalogue, so the existing permission ids are unchanged. Editor, viewer and auditor get no key routes. Open: last_used_at is kept per worker and not saved, so a restart clears it. |
| /api/dev/pprof* | handlers/devpprof.go | n/a | Dev-only profiling. |

### a4. Ops and transport

| Feature | Bifrost | Raiju status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| GET /health | handlers/health.go | done | `server.tov` `dispatch`. Returns `{"status":"ok"}`. Liveness only (L183): the store and listener checks are on GET /ready (Bifrost's /health pings its config, log and vector stores and answers 503 when one fails; Raiju's stores are files opened at start, which /ready reports). |
| GET /ready (readiness) | not in Bifrost (its /health pings the stores) | done (Raiju-only) | L183, `server.tov` `readyResponse` → `lifecycle.tov` `readyReport`: 503 `not_ready` with reasons while the process drains for a stop, or when logs_store or audit is enabled and could not open; components listener, config (a failed save is reported, not fatal), logs_store, audit. Broker mode answers `/ready` too (L189: 503 while it drains). Each worker answers for itself. In docs/openapi/management.yaml. |
| Graceful stop on SIGTERM and SIGINT | server/server.go Start (signal.Notify, fasthttp Shutdown, cleanup within 30 s) | done | L183, Tov patch 0012 (`Bun.onStopSignal`; the supervisor forwards the signal to each worker, closes its copy of the listener and exits 0 once the workers have) and `lifecycle.tov` `beginStop`, wired in `main.tov` for both servers: /ready turns 503, the listener closes after `server.shutdown_delay_seconds` (default 0), the requests and streams in hand get up to `server.shutdown_grace_seconds` (default 25) from the signal, then `ops.flushForStop` sends the exporter batches, retained content, trace spans and shared counters (within 5 s) and the process exits 0. L189: WebSocket and WebRTC sessions are part of the drain (`drain.tov` `OpenSessions`, registered by `realtime.tov` `SocketExt.track` for every WebSocket route on both servers and by `webrtc.tov` for WebRTC calls and live sessions): the UI's live feed sockets (`/ws`) are closed with 1001 (going away) when the listener closes (the dashboard resumes elsewhere from its cursor); realtime, Responses and GPT Live sessions run on until the grace and are then closed with a 1001 close frame, reason `server shutting down` (a WebRTC call is closed), so their usage is billed and their rows logged before the flush; what has not closed 3 s later is cut. Each worker drains its own. Broker mode (`-mode broker`) stops gracefully too (`main.tov` `runBroker`, `broker.tov` `startBroker`: `/ready` 503, the listener closed after the delay, the exchanges in hand within the grace, exit 0, and the server.* timeouts and connection cap). e2e `l189_e2e` (both servers, two bun workers, the broker). Differs: Bifrost's fasthttp Shutdown waits for every connection with no limit and closes the realtime sessions at the signal; Raiju lets the sessions run until the grace and cuts what is left after it. |
| GET /metrics | plugins/telemetry | done | `server.tov` `dispatch` → `ops.metricsText` → `metrics.tov`. Families and labels follow Bifrost (http, request, token, cost, cache, upstream, stream, overhead components, routing, key rotation, active requests, provider key up, MCP duration). Per-family label sets were not re-checked against Bifrost one by one. The raiju_* families are extras. L184: label values callers choose are bounded (see the http_requests_total row). L195: open by default as in Bifrost; Raiju's opt-ins `server.metrics_token` (a bearer token, `env.NAME` allowed; 401 without it, `server.tov` `metricsAllowed`) and `cluster.serve_metrics` (served on the cluster listener only) keep the tenant labels off the inference port. |
| GET / (web UI) | handlers/ui.go | done | `server.tov` `dispatch` and `uiResponse`, `uiserve.tov` `serveUi`, the build in `ui_assets.tov` (generated by `scripts/embed_ui.py` from `web/dist`, committed). Raiju's own UI, a subset of Bifrost's. L113: the admin client is a SolidJS 2 (release candidate) and TypeScript app in `web/`, built by Vite on Bun, with API types generated from `docs/openapi/management.yaml`. Screens: setup (L189: the first admin, with the setup token, while no admin exists), sign-in (admin token, SSO when `/api/oidc/status` says enabled), overview (health, circuit breakers, response cache, MCP clients, models of the last hour, `/metrics`), providers (add, edit, delete, keys), virtual keys (create, edit, rotate, enable, delete, budgets, rate limits, provider access, MCP tool lists), budgets, rate limits, teams and customers, logs (filters, time range, pages of 25, a detail with the bodies and raw payloads), the live feed, MCP clients (add, edit, delete, tool switches, reconnect), settings (PUT /api/config) and a playground. The page is served with `no-cache`, an ETag, a content security policy and 304; the hashed files under `/assets/` are immutable, with a gzip copy for clients that accept it; `RAIJU_UI_DIR` serves a directory instead. Plain CSS, light and dark, works at phone width, no network loads but the gateway. L132 added: dashboard charts (`web/src/charts.tsx`, small SVG components with no chart library: stacked bars and lines over time, ranked bars, a legend, a hover and arrow-key readout and a data table behind every chart; light and dark series colours): the overview draws requests and errors, latency percentiles, tokens, cost, cost by provider, top models and top providers for a chosen period from one `/api/logs/dashboard` call and `/api/logs/rankings/by-dimension`, and the logs screen draws the requests, a measure (cost, tokens, p95 latency) broken down by provider, model or virtual key, and the ranking of that dimension, for the log filters in force (the `/api/logs/histogram*` routes). New tabs: MCP access (the OAuth grants of the authorization server with filter and revoke; the per-user sessions with kind filter, re-authorize link and revoke; per-user headers submissions: open for a key, enter the values, verified on save) and Virtual MCPs (virtual MCP editor with tools, renames, descriptions and defaults, key attach and detach, resolved tools and errors; tool group editor in Bifrost's shape with keys, teams, customers and providers). Role-aware actions: the UI reads `GET /api/users/me/permissions` (`web/src/perm.ts`) and hides or disables what the role cannot do (built-in roles mirror `rbac.tov`; custom roles and management API keys use their pairs), hides the tabs a role cannot open and names the signed-in user and role in the header. The server still checks every request. `make web` rebuilds and embeds, `make web-check` fails when the committed files are stale. L141 added: chart zoom and series toggles (drag across a time chart, or hold Shift with the arrow keys and press Enter, to select a range: the overview sends `start_time` and `end_time` to `/api/logs/dashboard` in place of `period`, the logs screen applies the range to its filters, the list and the charts, and a Reset zoom button restores them; each legend entry is a button that hides or shows its series and the axis rescales), and a Roles tab (`web/src/screens/Roles.tsx`): the built-in roles read only, the custom roles with a permission grid (resource by operation, from `/api/resources` and `/api/operations`) to create, edit and delete them, access profiles attached to a role (the L103 routes), and the role each admin user holds, with every control following the caller's own permissions. Gaps: the group editor drops renames and defaults of a tool when it saves a group; the permission mirror for built-in roles is a copy of the rules in `rbac.tov`, not generated from them; the role editor leaves a role's per-resource data access overrides (`entity_dac`) as they were and does not create users or set an access profile's default flag. |
| CORS and preflight (`client.allowed_origins`) | lib/ | done | `server.tov` `cors` and `EXPOSED`. |
| POST /mcp (gateway as MCP server, sessions) | handlers/mcpserver.go | done | `ops.tov` `mcp` → `mcpserver.tov` `handleMcp`. L133: when the caller reaches code mode clients, tools/list adds the four code mode meta-tools and tools/call runs them (as Bifrost's tool list does); a virtual MCP (`/mcp/{slug}`) does not list them. |
| GET /mcp (SSE stream) and GET /mcp/{slug} | handlers/mcpserver.go | done | `ops.tov` `mcp` answers GET with `text/event-stream` after the same key and virtual-MCP checks as POST: the `connection/opened` event, then Bifrost's `: heartbeat` comment every 15 s (`mcpserver.tov` sseStream) until the client leaves; a client that is gone fails the next write and the stream is cancelled (the Bun server's pump, and `front.tov` for the http1 server, L166). DELETE ends a session. |
| POST /mcp/{slug} (virtual MCP) | handlers/mcpserver.go | done | `server.tov` `dispatch` → `ops.tov` `mcp` (virtual name) → `vmcp.tov`. |
| /ws (UI WebSocket) | handlers/websocket.go | done | `server.tov` `wsUpgrade` → `ops.tov` `UiSocket` and `UiHub` (log feed, scoped to the caller's data access). Accepts `?ticket=` from /api/session/ws-ticket (L97c). Heartbeat, and a registry shared across worker processes (L102). L119: the feed is cluster-wide and resumable (the Raiju-only row in (h2)): `?since=node:seq,...` replays what the client missed on any replica, `?batch=1` sends arrays every 100 ms, and a slow batch client is closed with 1013. The SolidJS Live screen (L113, `web/src/live.ts`) asks for `batch=1`, keeps the highest seq per producer and resumes with `since=` on every reconnect (backoff 1 s doubling to 30 s), shows the gap notice and a node column and filter, keeps the newest 5,000 rows (newest on top, pause and resume, duplicates by event id dropped) and draws them through a fixed-row-height virtualizer (`web/src/virtual.ts`; @tanstack/solid-virtual does not accept solid-js 2), so an event adds one row and rebuilds none. L168: store updates go through the feed too (`ops.tov` `storeUpdated`, `feed.tov` kind store_update): each takes the producer's next sequence, reaches every worker's and node's clients whatever their data scope, and is in the replay ring, so a client that resumes with since= gets the store updates it missed. They keep Bifrost's {"type":"store_update","tags":[...]} shape, with id, node and seq added. |
| HTTP/1.1 server and Bun.serve | n/a | done (Raiju-only) | `front.tov`, `server.tov` `start`. L183: both apply `server.header_timeout_seconds` (10; a half-sent head is answered 408, a silent new connection closed), `body_timeout_seconds` (60 without a body byte: 408), `idle_timeout_seconds` (60, a keep-alive connection with no request in hand) and `max_connections` per process (default three quarters of the descriptor limit; past it a request is answered 503); a request being handled, a slow completion or a stream, is never timed out. Bun.serve has them in the runtime (Tov patch 0012, which also answers 503 from a spare descriptor when out of descriptors instead of spinning on the listener), the http1 server in `http1.tov` (`Http1Server`, `expiry`). A client that stops reading is dropped: output the client does not take for `idle_timeout_seconds` ends the connection, whatever it is carrying (Bun.serve in the runtime since 0012, for HTTP connections; the http1 server since L189, `http1.tov` `stalled`, for every connection including WebSockets, by the bytes the kernel has taken). e2e `l189_stall`. |
| Crew routing offline priors and classifier training (Raiju-only) | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | L152, `scripts/crew_priors.py`: `fit-priors` (role-additive fit with a metadata prior, leaderboard moments, optional interactions, per-candidate IRT by marginal likelihood when numpy and scipy are installed), `train-classifier` (the file format of `src/taskclass.tov`, int8, at most 2,000 weights per class), `frontier` (class frontiers and knee). Tests in `tests/test_crew_priors.py`. The gateway loads the priors and classifier files (L153: `crew.tov` loadPriorsFile and `taskclass.tov` loadClassifier, re-read on change). |

| x-bf-agent-vk-id, x-bf-agent-auth | cli/internal/client/client.go; handlers/agentgateway.go | done | L134. Bifrost's CLI sends `Authorization: Bearer <agent token>` with `x-bf-agent-vk-id` (the assigned virtual key it picked) and reads `x-bf-agent-auth` on a 401 as "the agent credential was refused, sign in again" (the server side is Enterprise and not in the OSS source). Raiju: `governance.tov` `selectedKey` and `userOf` (set by `ops.tov` from the admin users): a request whose Authorization names a user and that carries x-bf-agent-vk-id runs as the key with that id and `user_id` equal to that user; a key assigned to someone else, or an unknown id, is a 403 naming it (`unassignedSelection`); a request that already carries a virtual key ignores the header. `server.tov` `markAgentRejection` adds `x-bf-agent-auth: rejected` to a 401 on a request that looks like the CLI's (x-bf-agent-vk-id, X-Bifrost-Agent, or a `ck-bf-agent-` bearer) when neither a key nor a user accepted its bearer; other refusals (403s for models, budgets, someone else's key) carry no mark. L194: the Enterprise agent-token endpoints the CLI calls (`agentsignin.tov`, `ops.tov` agentRoute; Bifrost's server side is not in its OSS source, so the shapes are the ones cli/internal/browserauth, sessionauth and agentapi read): GET /api/agent/auth/status ({idp_configured, virtual_key_auth_enabled}); GET /api/agent/auth/authorize (client_id, an http loopback redirect_uri with a port, state, an S256 code_challenge) serves a sign-in page that takes a user's password or a dashboard single sign-on session (a link through /api/oidc/login comes back to it), and POST answers 302 to the redirect with a one-time code (two minutes) and the state; POST /api/agent/auth/token trades the code, its PKCE verifier and the same redirect for the CLI's TokenResponse (agent_access_token `ck-bf-agent-...`, one hour; refresh_token, 30 days; the user; selected_virtual_key_id, the user's default key; forced_virtual_key_id empty), OAuth's invalid_grant otherwise (a code works once, right or wrong); POST /api/agent/auth/refresh rotates the pair (a reused refresh token ends its family); POST /api/agent/auth/logout revokes it; GET /api/agent/virtual-keys (the user's active assigned keys without values, their providers and budgets) and /api/agent/usage-summary (the keys' and the user's @user budgets, the keys' rate limits) take the agent token, refused with 401 and x-bf-agent-auth: rejected. On inference an agent token names its user (`governance.tov` userOf, altAuth): with x-bf-agent-vk-id it runs as that assigned key, and without it as the user's first active, unexpired assigned key in config order (`defaultKey`, the CLI's "gateway's deterministic default"); a user with no assigned key runs as the user, as before. A disabled user's tokens stop working. Codes and tokens live in the process's memory (by hash), as dashboard sessions do (oidc.tov UiSessions): with bun workers each worker holds its own, and a restart signs the CLI out (it signs in again on the x-bf-agent-auth mark). No key is forced (Bifrost Enterprise's admin-forced key has no config here). e2e `l194_e2e`. |

Credential-bearing headers are recorded as REDACTED; Bifrost stores them as sent. |

| x-bifrost-passthrough-path | responseheaders.go:130 | done | Passthrough routes answer with the provider path they forwarded to, without the passthrough prefix (`passthrough.tov` passthroughRoute). |

| x-bifrost-retry-after-ms | responseheaders.go:132 | done | `gateway.tov` serveInner, on errors when the upstream gave a retry hint. |
| `extra_fields` in the body, or in headers with `client.extra_fields_placement` | lib/responseheaders.go:161-170; config.schema.json client | done | Body by default: raw request and response go in `extra_fields` when send-back is on (`gateway.tov` withRawFields). With `client.extra_fields_placement: "headers"` the body has no `extra_fields`, and the scalars go out as headers (latency, converted and dropped compat, passthrough path, error type, retry hint). The raw fields have no header and are dropped, as Bifrost does. |
| Access-Control-Expose-Headers for the x-bifrost-* headers | lib/responseheaders.go ExposedResponseHeaders | done | `server.tov` EXPOSED. |
| Webhook delivery headers: webhook-id, webhook-timestamp, webhook-signature, x-bifrost-event | framework/configstore/tables/webhooks.go:100-101; framework/webhooks/client.go:115 | done | `webhooks.tov` (signed Standard Webhooks deliveries; these names cannot be overridden by custom headers). |
| x-bf-attempts | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` attempt and serveInner. |
| x-bf-fallback-used | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` serveInner. |
| x-bf-overhead-us | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` serveInner. |
| x-bf-cache (hit or semantic-hit) | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` serveInner. |
| x-bf-plugin-short-circuit, x-bf-agent-steps, x-bf-agent-tool-calls | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` chatOnce and chat. |

## (c) config.json

### c1. Top-level sections

Bifrost: top-level properties of `transports/config.schema.json`. Raiju: `Config` in `src/config.tov`, the Bifrost-to-Raiju mapping in `src/config_compat.tov` (`normalize`), and the startup notes in `config_compat.tov` (`noteTable`, which says why each ignored key is ignored). Keys Raiju does not act on are reported at startup, so none are dropped silently.

| Feature | Bifrost | Raiju status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| version, source_of_truth, env_label | config.schema.json (top) | done | Accepted silently (`config_compat.tov` topKeys); no effect, as Raiju reads its config file as written (L97g). |
| $schema | config.schema.json (top) | done | Accepted and skipped (`config_compat.tov` topKeys). |
| encryption_key | config.schema.json (top) | done | Done with a different key derivation: `store.tov` cipherFromConfig seals provider key values, virtual key values, and the admin password and token with AES-256-GCM. The key is PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 (100,000 rounds, fixed salt); Bifrost uses Argon2id, so sealed files do not open in the other product. `env.NAME` is allowed. L147: the token_exchange admin credentials are sealed too, and they travel on the cluster state sealed, so every node of a cluster must hold the same encryption_key (a node without it cannot open a credential another node renewed). Differs internally: Raiju never opens Bifrost's sealed store, so the key derivation is not a compatibility point. (commits ae6f1db, G39) |
| setup_token | config.schema.json (top) | done | While no admin account exists, `POST /api/users` needs the `X-Bifrost-Setup-Token` header when setup_token is set (`ops.tov` users, L97g), or the setup session cookie. `POST /api/session/setup` (L97l: the branch in `ops.tov` session routing; the cookie in `setupsession.tov` `issueSetupSession`, `validSetupSession`) trades the token for an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict cookie valid 12 hours, 403 for a wrong token and 409 once an admin exists. `/api/session/is-auth-enabled` reports `setup_required` and `setup_token_configured`. env.NAME is allowed. The one-time bootstrap token is not used. L172: with setup_token set, the setup lock covers every other /api route as Bifrost's does (see the /api/session/setup row). L184: with no setup_token the API is locked too (403), as in Bifrost, and `BIFROST_SETUP_TOKEN` stands in for an unset setup_token. L189: the web UI has a setup screen (`web/src/screens/Setup.tsx`), shown instead of the dashboard while `/api/session/is-auth-enabled` says `setup_required`: the setup token is checked with `POST /api/session/setup` (which sets the setup session cookie, as Bifrost's dashboard does), the first admin is created with `POST /api/users` and `X-Bifrost-Setup-Token`, and its token is shown once and signs the tab in; with no setup token configured the screen explains how to set one, as Bifrost's `SetupTokenView`. `make ui` covers it (a second gateway with no admin). Differs: Bifrost's screen then sends the operator to its security settings to create a user with a password; Raiju's admins are token users, so the screen creates the admin itself. |
| server.* (read_buffer_size, a2a_*, plugin_download_*, webrtc_enabled) | config.schema.json server | done (accepted) | Accepted (L97g). Raiju takes `-host` and `-port` on the command line; the other keys have no Raiju counterpart, except `a2a_allow_private_push_callbacks` (L134, `config.tov` ServerSectionConfig): push callbacks may then go to private addresses (metadata addresses stay refused); L186: the callback's host is resolved when a config is set and before each send, and every address is checked (`a2apush.tov` `callbackConnectProblem`). a2a_grpc_base_domain and a2a_grpc_port enable the A2A gRPC listener (L148, `config.tov` ServerSectionConfig and `a2agrpc.tov`): the listener starts only when both are set and fails the start if it cannot bind; RAIJU_A2A_GRPC_LISTEN_PORT overrides the bind port, and the cards keep advertising a2a_grpc_port. `plugin_download_private_allowlist` (L170, `config.tov` ServerSectionConfig, `plugindl.tov` `urlProblem`) lets a plugin URL reach the private hosts it names (metadata addresses stay refused); L186: entries may be hosts, addresses or CIDR ranges, and each hop's host is resolved and every address checked. Raiju-only lifecycle keys (L183, `lifecycle.tov`, known to `config_compat.tov`): `shutdown_grace_seconds`, `shutdown_delay_seconds`, `header_timeout_seconds`, `idle_timeout_seconds`, `body_timeout_seconds`, `max_connections` (the graceful stop and the HTTP servers' timeouts and cap, read at start; see (a4)). `webrtc_enabled` (L184, Raiju-only: Bifrost has no such key) turns on the WebRTC calls terminated on the gateway's own peer; off by default (`live.tov` `webrtcRefusal`). L188: push callbacks and plugin downloads are also checked by the runtime when each connection is made (`tov-patches/0013`). |
| auth_config (admin_username, admin_password, is_enabled, disable_auth_on_inference) | config.schema.json auth_config | done | P0 (G2). `config_compat.tov` mapAuth; `config.tov` AdminAuthConfig. `is_enabled: false` turns admin auth off, and `disable_auth_on_inference` leaves inference open unless `client.enforce_auth_on_inference` says otherwise. |
| client.* | config.schema.json client | partial | See c2. |
| providers.* | config.schema.json providers | partial | See c3. |
| governance.* | config.schema.json governance | done | See c4. |
| alerting | config.schema.json alerting | done | `config_compat.tov` mapAlerting; rules as CEL (commit 8e98ea5). history_retention_days and webhook_network are reported as ignored. L195: `env.NAME` is resolved in a channel's `url`/`webhook_url`, `routing_key` and header values, as Bifrost's alerting docs show. |
| webhooks[] | config.schema.json webhooks | done | `config.tov` WebhookEndpoint; `webhooks.tov` (Standard Webhooks signing, retries, `allow_private_network`). L186: each attempt checks the host's resolved addresses (`connectProblem`, `netguard.tov`: canonical address forms, link-local and metadata always refused, private ones only with `allow_private_network`) and redirects are not followed, as Bifrost's webhook client does. Deliveries are kept (at most the last 500; persisted with logs_store, L97i) and older than `client.webhook_config.delivery_history_retention_days` (default 30, L97g). L188: the runtime checks the addresses again when each connection is made (fetch's `peer` option, `tov-patches/0013-fetch-peer-policy.patch`), so a DNS answer that changes after the check (rebinding) is refused too. |
| agents[] | config.schema.json agents | done | `config.tov` `AgentConfig`, read into the stored registrations (`store.tov` setAgents, the same list /api/agents edits), with env references and Bifrost's object-form values for headers, the OAuth client and the TLS certificates (`config_compat.tov` mapAgents). L134: runtime_auth and discovery_auth take type none, headers or oauth (client credentials with token_url, discovery_url or provider_url, scopes and resource: `agentauth.tov`), security_schemes and advanced are kept, tls takes insecure_skip_verify and ca_cert_pem (the client certificate and key are kept but not used: Tov's fetch has no client certificates), and forward_accepted_credential and forward_accepted_credential_overrides_auth are applied. L171: the cards of declared agents are fetched at start (one process of a node) and on the agent's first call (L171, as Bifrost discovers lazily). Declarations are reconciled as Bifrost's loadAgentsConfig does: a name declared twice is skipped (both copies) and a declaration with a bad name or card URL is skipped, each with a warning at start (`config.tov` servedAgents, agentDeclarationNotes); Raiju's config file is its store, so a registration made through the API is a declaration in the file and source_of_truth split and config.json come to the same. extension_uris (`a2aext.tov`) and tenant are read, shown and applied (tenant is kept, as Bifrost keeps it without sending it). L190: the client certificate and key of tls are presented to the agent (card, token calls, JSON-RPC, REST and gRPC; Tov patch 0015), and a certificate without its key (or the reverse) fails the agent's calls with Bifrost's message; e2e `l190_e2e` against agents that require mutual TLS. |
| mcp.* | config.schema.json mcp | done | See c5 (its last open row, openapi_config, closed in L177). |
| vector_store | config.schema.json vector_store | done | Types qdrant, redis, weaviate, pinecone and chromem are mapped (`config_compat.tov` mapVectorStore; G30): all five of Bifrost's types (`config.schema.json` vector_store enum). Bifrost's connection-pool and timeout fields are reported as ignored (L97g LIMITS; differs internally: Raiju's own connection handling). A type outside this list is dropped, and the semantic cache keeps entries in memory. L188: the HTTP stores (Qdrant, Weaviate, Pinecone) may be on a private network; link-local, unspecified and metadata addresses are refused when the runtime connects (`vectorstore.tov` STORE_PEER; Bifrost checks none). Redis and Valkey go over raw sockets and are not checked. |
| config_store (sqlite, postgres) | config.schema.json config_store | done (owner decision: JSON config file) | Raiju saves its config as a JSON file (the file given by `-config`), with a `.bak` (`store.tov`). Saves are atomic (L158, `atomicfile.tov` on Tov patch 0009): the file is written to a temporary file in the same folder, flushed, renamed over config.json and the folder flushed, so a crash or another worker never sees half a file and the file keeps its permissions; temporary files a crash left are removed at startup once ten minutes old. The other whole-file state (MCP OAuth state, audit key, anchor and archive mark, webhook deliveries, A2A push files, crew state, the catalog, log and vector-store rewrites, the workers' share files and caches) is written the same way; append-only logs still append. `config_store` is reported as not used. Design (owner decision 5 after wave 22): the JSON file is the config store; no sqlite or postgres store. Every save puts a generation (the text's sha256) on the cluster state (L185, `store.tov` `CONFIG_NS`, `takeGeneration`): the other workers of the node reread the file, and a management API change is answered once they all run it (`ops.tov` `settleConfig`), as Bifrost reloads from its shared store after a write; other nodes reread a shared file or pull the text (`cluster.config_sync`, docs/operations.md). |
| logs_store.* | config.schema.json logs_store | done (owner decision: JSONL log store) | Raiju's own block: enabled, dir, retention_days, max_memory_entries, content_logging, max_file_mb, max_disk_mb (`config.tov` LogsStoreConfig; `logstore.tov` prunes by retention_days at start and then every minute while rows are written). L182: a single process writes its rows in batches (every 100 ms or 64 KB, as Bifrost's batch writer, which flushes every 5 s or 1,000 rows; the workers of a node still write row by row, since they read each other's rows from the files), a day file is cut into segments of max_file_mb (default 32), start-up reads only the newest segments of today (a segment over four times that size is skipped with a note), and max_disk_mb (default 0, no cap; Bifrost's SQL stores have none) deletes the oldest segments first; a failing disk is reported at most every 10 s. object_storage (L125) backs retained content and, since L193, the hybrid store: each row's payload goes to the bucket (see retain_content_in_object_storage; object_storage_exclude_fields, object_storage_exclude_request_types, object_storage.format and compression as Bifrost's, plus Raiju's object_storage_offload: false to keep rows whole); its bucket and credentials may be secret reference objects (L168). Bifrost's `type` (sqlite, postgres, clickhouse) and `config` are not used. Log sinks are configured as plugins or Raiju's `log_exports` and `exporters` (see plugins[]). Design (owner decision 5 after wave 22): the JSONL file store, no sqlite, postgres or clickhouse store. Earlier days are read back from the day files (L179); L193: rows beyond max_memory_entries are read by a paged scan, and a node answers with its peers' rows too (cluster.log_reads; see (h2) Request log store). L185: dir (default `logs`) is read from the app dir, not the working directory (`config.tov` `withAppDirs`), as Bifrost keeps logs.db in its app dir; an enabled store whose folder cannot be written stops the start, as Bifrost's start fails when its log store does not open (`required: false` starts without it; `ops.tov` `storageProblems`). |
| skills_registry | config.schema.json skills_registry | out-of-scope | The skills marketplace is in SCOPE.md Out (L88). Reported as ignored. |
| plugins[] | config.schema.json plugins | done | Mapped by `config_compat.tov` mapPlugins: otel, logging, telemetry, governance, routing, compat, prompts, semantic_cache (or semanticcache), and the sink plugins bigquery, pubsub, kafka and maxim (`mapSinkPlugin`, which turns them into exporters). Other entries stay in `plugins` for the plugin chain (`plugins.tov` buildChain): mocker, jsonparser (its options checked by `config_compat.tov` noteJsonParser) and a WebAssembly plugin given by `path` (L121); names the chain does not know are reported and not loaded. L174: `model-catalog-resolver` (Bifrost's plugin name; `modelcatalogresolver` too) is accepted and taken out of plugins[] with a note: Raiju resolves a bare model's provider from the model catalog on every request, preferring the integration route's provider (`router.tov` parseModelVia; `config_compat.tov` mapCatalogResolver), as Bifrost registers the resolver on its own whenever a catalog exists; enabled false cannot turn it off, as Bifrost has no switch for it either. |
| websocket | config.schema.json websocket | done | max_connections_per_user caps the open sessions of each route: /v1/responses, /v1/realtime and /v1/live/sessions each count their own (`ws.tov` SocketGate, wired in `realtime.tov` and `server.tov`; L112a). A socket past the cap gets Bifrost's 429 refusal and is closed (L172): on /v1/responses `{"type":"error","status":429,"error":{"code":"websocket_connection_limit_reached","message":"websocket connection limit reached"}}` (handlers/wsresponses.go writeWSError), on the realtime and live routes the realtime error event with type and code `rate_limit_exceeded` (wsrealtime.go, wslive.go) (`realtime.tov` `fullEvent`, `wsresponses.tov`). pool and transcript_buffer_size are accepted and not applied, by design: Bifrost reads transcript_buffer_size nowhere, and Raiju opens one upstream socket per session, so there is no pool to reuse. The /ws live feed is not counted (Bifrost does not count its UI socket either). e2e `l112a_e2e`. |
| guardrails_config | config.schema.json guardrails_config | done | `guardrails_compat.tov`; `main.tov` withBifrostGuardrails (commit 0c999ff). The secrets engine of L101 (capture groups, entropy filter, allowlists, stopwords and generic-api-key) is in place. The full Gitleaks set is in place from L93. |
| audit_logs | config.schema.json audit_logs | done | Read as `audit` (`config_compat.tov` mapAudit, L97p, L104). Every key of Bifrost's audit_logs_config is mapped: disabled (as enabled), hmac_key (key), retention_days, omit_ip_addresses, archive_grace_period (archive_grace_seconds), object_storage with archive_interval (archive and its interval_seconds; compress as archive_compress) and archive_max_object_bytes. The archive's remaining gaps are in (f) Audit logs. L185: `audit.dir` (default `logs/audit`) is read from the app dir; a log that cannot open stops the start unless `audit.required` is false, and failed appends count in `raiju_audit_write_errors_total`. |
| cluster_config | config.schema.json cluster_config | done | Read as `cluster` (`config_compat.tov` mapCluster). `type: broker` is supported as Raiju's HTTP relay (`-mode broker`, L94), not Bifrost's gRPC relay. Discovery and leader election are in (L87, L92, L94). region, gossip and grpc are reported as ignored. Raiju-only `cluster.config_sync` (L185: file, pull or off) sets how a node takes another node's config saves. L195: `env.NAME` is resolved in `secret`, `bind`, `broker.address` and `discovery.advertise_address` / `consul_address` (`config.tov` resolveClusterAndTelemetry); Raiju-only `listen_port`, `bind` and `serve_metrics` (the cluster listener) and `log_reads` are read, not flagged. Differs internally: Raiju's discovery and sync wire replace Bifrost's gossip and gRPC relay; the state a cluster shares (budgets, rate limits, key holds, MCP and A2A state) is kept on every node, as in Bifrost (see (i) Clustering). |
| load_balancer_config | config.schema.json load_balancer_config | done | Mapped into `health.adaptive` (`config_compat.tov` mapHealth). L165: direction_selection_enabled, append_fallbacks_to_pinned, reroute_failed_directions and prune_failed_fallbacks are read into health and applied at the provider level (`router.tov` directRefs, directionHealthy, eligibleProviders; `gateway.tov` after the routing rules): a direction is broken when every key serving the model is held or has an open breaker. A request that names no provider takes a healthy provider for its model (the one it resolved to when healthy) with the other healthy ones behind its fallbacks; a pinned request is untouched unless append adds the healthy providers for its model, or reroute replaces a broken primary (kept when none is healthy); prune drops broken fallbacks where the list is rebuilt; a pinned key (x-bf-api-key) is never moved. L180: `enabled` alone turns adaptive balancing on, and route_selection_enabled is read as `health.route_selection`: false picks keys by their static weights (`router.tov` pickHealthy) while held keys and open breakers are still skipped and the provider level stays on, as Bifrost documents. The provider level ranks providers by measured performance (`router.tov` rankDirections, directionScore; `health.tov` performance: the best usable key's success rate squared over its latency EWMA): a request that names no provider takes the best healthy one (the provider it resolved to wins a tie), a provider not tried yet goes first so it is measured, and the appended fallbacks follow the same order. tracker_config and bootstrap are noted as ignored: by design, since no Go code in Bifrost OSS reads them (schema only). |
| circuit_breaker_config | config.schema.json circuit_breaker_config | done | Mapped to `health.circuit_breaker` (`config_compat.tov` mapHealth). Breaker policies (`circuit_breaker_policies`, L91) are Raiju's own. |
| large_payload_optimization | config.schema.json large_payload_optimization | partial | `largepayload.tov` (settings with Bifrost's defaults: request and response thresholds 10 MiB, prefetch 64 KiB, max payload 500 MiB, truncated log 1 MiB; the prefix scanner), `gateway.tov` `largeRequest`/`passThrough`, `server.tov` `largeFront` (Bun.serve) and `front.tov` + `http1.tov` BodyQueue (the http1 server); L124 on Tov patch 0008 (a streamed `fetch` body, `Request.body` in Bun.serve, `Socket.pause`). On `/v1/chat/completions`, `/v1/completions`, `/v1/embeddings`, `/v1/responses` and `/anthropic/v1/messages` (and their `/openai` aliases), a body over request_threshold_bytes (its Content-Length, or what a chunked body has sent) is read for the first prefetch_size_bytes: Bifrost's LargePayloadMetadata (model, stream flag, generationConfig.responseModalities and speechConfig, thread.type) is taken from them, the model is rewritten to the provider's name inside the prefix, and the whole body is streamed to the provider (chunked, or with the caller's Content-Length adjusted; HTTP/2 DATA frames over enforce_http2) while the gateway holds at most a few hundred KiB of it (the e2e checks a 48 MiB body moves the gateway's peak memory by under 24 MiB, on both servers). A body over max_payload_bytes is a 413 (a declared length at once, a chunked body when it passes the limit). Skipped for such a request, as Bifrost skips them: input guardrails and the prompt, plugins and complexity routing, the exact and semantic caches, compat shaping (but a text completion's drops and fits, L178), injected tools and the MCP agent loop, raw request logging, fallbacks and retries (the body is spent); routing rules see only model and stream. The request log keeps truncated_log_bytes of the body (and of the answer). An answer over response_threshold_bytes (Content-Length), or an event stream, goes back as it arrives without being read whole; an ordinary request's answer over the threshold does too unless output guardrails or plugins have to see it. Which provider takes the body as it is depends on the route (`takesBodyAsIs` in `gateway.tov`): OpenAI-format providers for chat, completions and embeddings; OpenAI for Responses (`/v1/responses`, as the buffered route sends Responses natively to `openai` only); Anthropic for Messages (the route's native passthrough, which only renames the model). Every other provider whose format differs is **converted while the body streams in** (L136, next paragraph) when its route has a converter; the rest (completions to a provider that is not OpenAI-format when compat does not send them as chat, Replicate aside (the whole-body route refuses those too); Anthropic with `prompt_cache.cache_control_injection_points`, whose points name messages by index from either end and apply only if no message carries `cache_control`; a Bedrock key with `use_openai_endpoints`; any bare model that resolves to a provider without a converter) get the body buffered up to client.max_request_body_size_mb and converted as before (413 past the size limit, with the reason in the message). **Providers whose format differs (L136)**: a large chat request to Anthropic, Gemini, Cohere, Bedrock (Converse) or Vertex (Claude and Gemini), and a large Messages request to Bedrock (InvokeModel) or Vertex Claude, is converted as it arrives and sent on without being held. `jsonstream.tov` is a streaming JSON tokenizer (bytes in any chunking; keys, numbers and strings up to 4 KiB whole; a longer string as a head, pieces and an end, so a base64 image of any size is never collected; the JSON grammar is checked as it goes). `streamconv.tov` reads the chat messages with it and writes the target body as it goes (`ChatConv`: message text and image data are copied through as the raw JSON string bytes the caller wrote, which is the same JSON though not always the same escapes; `MessagesConv` for the Messages route, where only top-level fields change: no `model` or `stream`, plus `anthropic_version`, copied everything else). `largeconv.tov` picks the converter per route and provider (`familyFor`), builds the upstream request (`upstreamFor`; the providers' `largeRequest` functions) and runs the conversion as the provider's request body (`convertedStream`) while the request log keeps the truncated copy of what the caller sent. The parameters and tail fields come from the provider modules' own functions (`messagesTail`, `generateTail`, `converseTail`, `chatTail`), shared with the whole-body converters, so thinking budgets, max_tokens, tools, tool_choice, stop, response_format and the rest are decided in one place. The unit tests compare the streaming output with the whole-body converters on a corpus of 20 requests (system messages anywhere, developer role, tool calls and results, images, parts of other kinds, odd key orders, empty messages) for five formats, with and without auto caching, with the request arriving a byte at a time or whole and with a 16-byte string limit: the JSON is equal (keys sorted). **What is held**: the small top-level fields, system messages (they are hoisted: Anthropic's `system`, Gemini's `systemInstruction`, Converse's `system`, written after the messages, so system messages anywhere in the list work with no look-ahead; the key order of Anthropic's body is therefore messages, system, parameters, where the whole-body converter writes system first, and Gemini, Converse and Cohere bodies are byte-identical for a request with its system first), tool call arguments, and, when the order is not the usual one, one message or one content part. A message streams when its `role` (and a tool message's `tool_call_id`) comes before its `content`, and a text or image part when its `type` comes before its data; otherwise that message (or part) is held whole and replayed in order. Anything held is bounded by client.max_request_body_size_mb, past which the request is refused with a 400 that names what was too large and how to order the JSON. Turn merging, Anthropic's auto caching (the newest block is closed last so it can take `cache_control`), Converse's cache points, Gemini's function names for tool results and Cohere's in-place system messages follow the whole-body converters. The converted body goes out chunked (its length is not known up front) except for Bedrock. **Bedrock** signs the SHA-256 of the payload (SigV4), and its runtime API takes neither a streaming signature nor an unsigned payload (those are S3's; Bifrost does not stream large payloads to Bedrock either), so the converted bytes are written to segment files in `$TMPDIR` (default /tmp; a directory `raiju-spool-*`, removed when the request ends, stale ones older than 6 hours removed when the next one starts) with an incremental SHA-256 (`crypto.tov` `Sha256`), then signed (`signHashed`) and sent with their length: the gateway's memory stays flat and its disk holds the converted body, up to max_payload_bytes. Because that happens before the request is authorised, a gateway that enforces virtual keys refuses a Bedrock request without one first. All keys of the Bedrock provider must resolve the model to the same id (a key alias that differs sends the request to the buffered path), since the reasoning fields depend on it. **The answer**: a non-streamed answer is small and goes through the ordinary translation and accounting (budgets, token limits, metrics, request log). A chat request that asks for a stream (the flag must lie in the first prefetch_size_bytes, as for the other large routes) is sent to Anthropic, Gemini, Cohere and Vertex as a stream request and the events are converted to chat chunks as they arrive by the usual stream converters (usage from the stream, as for any streamed answer; the chunk with usage follows the caller's `stream_options.include_usage`, read from the body once it is in); for Bedrock, ConverseStream is called and its binary event-stream frames are converted to chat chunks as they arrive (L162; the spooled body is signed as for Converse). The Messages route returns the provider's own Anthropic-format answer as it came (a Vertex Claude stream is passed through and its usage read from its two ends, as L129). Not covered: a body whose big strings are in fields other than message text, image URLs and tool calls is held up to the limit; `prompt_cache.cache_control_injection_points` (buffered, above); injected tools, MCP tools, compat shaping and guardrails are skipped for a large request as for the other large routes; a body is not retried or sent to a fallback provider of another format (400); Anthropic's input `cache_control` blocks inside message content are not carried (as in the whole-body converter); chunked request bodies need a provider or proxy that accepts them (HTTP/2 uses DATA frames). **Responses requests (L145)**: a large `/v1/responses` request to a provider other than OpenAI is converted to chat as it streams in. `respconv.tov` (`RespConv`) reads the Responses body with `jsonstream.tov` and writes the chat request that `responses.toChatRequest` writes for the same body: the top-level fields go through `toChatRequest` whole (they are small); `instructions` lead as the system message when they come before `input` (else they follow the messages before them); a message streams when its `role` (and `type`, when there is one) comes before its `content`, a function output when its `call_id` comes before its `output`, and a content part when its `type` comes before its text or image URL; text, image data and function output strings flow through as the raw JSON bytes; an item or part that cannot stream is held whole (up to client.max_request_body_size_mb) and mapped the way `toChatRequest`'s item loop maps it. Parts and items that `toChatRequest` drops (input files, reasoning, built-in tool calls) are dropped here too, and `previous_response_id` is ignored, as it is on the whole-request path. The chat body then goes through the provider's chat converter (`familyFor` with the `resp-` prefix: Anthropic, Gemini, Vertex, Cohere, Bedrock Converse, held in `newConv` as a chain), or, for an OpenAI-format provider (`streamsNatively`, Azure included), goes out as it is to that provider's chat endpoint (`resp-openai`), with `stream_options.include_usage` when the caller streams. A request that does not stream calls the provider without streaming, as the whole-request path calls it, and its answer goes back whole (`toResponse`); its usage settles the budgets and the log as for any held request (`translate` in `gateway.tov`). A Responses stream (L178) calls the provider as a stream (the chat converters' stream tail and stream URL, as for a large chat stream), its events are converted to chat chunks by the usual stream converters and the chunks to Responses events as they arrive (`responses.tov` `ChatToResponses`, `StreamConverter.asResponses`), so the first text reaches the caller before the provider is done; the chunks carry their usage, which `response.completed` reports and the budgets and the log count. e2e `l178_e2e` (a 12 MiB Responses stream to Anthropic whose first text arrives before the provider's stream ends, and one to an OpenAI-format provider). The unit tests compare the streamed chat body with `toChatRequest` on a corpus of Responses bodies, split at every byte and in 1, 3 and 7 byte pieces, with 16 and 4096 byte string limits, and the chained conversion with the whole-body converters for five families. The e2e (`l145_e2e`): 12 MiB Responses requests to Anthropic, Gemini and an OpenAI-format provider arrive intact by content hash; a 48 MiB image to Anthropic and to Gemini moves the gateway's memory by under 24 MiB; a streamed Responses request is answered with Responses events; a converted answer is counted in the key's budget; instructions over the hold limit are a 400. Not covered: an output array of a function call (its parts are held, not streamed); instructions after the input follow the messages before them (the whole-request path puts them first, which matters only for Cohere's message order and the order of system prompts). **Messages and embeddings (L146)**: a large Messages request to OpenAI-format providers becomes a chat body as it streams in (`msgconv.tov`, `MsgToChat`: written in the order and form `toChatRequest` writes them, with `chatParams` for the parameters); to Gemini (and Vertex's Gemini) and to Cohere it is chained into the chat converter of that format (`MsgChain`, `ChatConv`), so those bodies are the whole-body ones. The answer comes back in Messages format, and a stream as Messages events (`StreamConverter.asAnthropic`, as the whole-body route does). A large `/v1/embeddings` to Gemini, Vertex, Cohere and Bedrock Cohere (`embconv.tov`, `EmbConv`) streams each input into the provider's batch body: Gemini's one `batchEmbedContents` request per input (inputs before `dimensions` are held, bounded, and written at the end), Vertex's `instances` (the size at the end), Cohere's `texts`; Bedrock's Cohere body is signed through the spool as Bedrock's is. Cloudflare and Databricks take the OpenAI embeddings body under their own URLs, as they are. Streamed where the keys in front of a long string say what it is: `role` before `content`; `type` before `text` (and before the image source, whose `type` and `media_type` come before `data` or `url`); `tool_use_id` before a tool result's content; the request's `system` as a string streams, as an array it is held (up to client.max_request_body_size_mb). Unit tests compare the streamed chat body with `toChatRequest` and the Gemini and Cohere bodies with their whole-body converters over a corpus of 15 requests at every chunk size and string limit, and the embeddings bodies with the whole-body ones; e2e (`l146_e2e`): a 40 MiB Messages request to OpenAI-format, Gemini (streamed too) and Cohere arrives intact by content hash and its answer is in Messages format, a 40 MiB embeddings request to Gemini arrives as one batch body, with no memory growth on either. **Providers whose format differs (L136)**: a large chat request to Anthropic, Gemini, Cohere, Bedrock (Converse) or Vertex (Claude and Gemini), and a large Messages request to Bedrock (InvokeModel) or Vertex Claude, is converted as it arrives and sent on without being held. `jsonstream.tov` is a streaming JSON tokenizer (bytes in any chunking; keys, numbers and strings up to 4 KiB whole; a longer string as a head, pieces and an end, so a base64 image of any size is never collected; the JSON grammar is checked as it goes). `streamconv.tov` reads the chat messages with it and writes the target body as it goes (`ChatConv`: message text and image data are copied through as the raw JSON string bytes the caller wrote, which is the same JSON though not always the same escapes; `MessagesConv` for the Messages route, where only top-level fields change: no `model` or `stream`, plus `anthropic_version`, copied everything else). `largeconv.tov` picks the converter per route and provider (`familyFor`), builds the upstream request (`upstreamFor`; the providers' `largeRequest` functions) and runs the conversion as the provider's request body (`convertedStream`) while the request log keeps the truncated copy of what the caller sent. The parameters and tail fields come from the provider modules' own functions (`messagesTail`, `generateTail`, `converseTail`, `chatTail`), shared with the whole-body converters, so thinking budgets, max_tokens, tools, tool_choice, stop, response_format and the rest are decided in one place. The unit tests compare the streaming output with the whole-body converters on a corpus of 20 requests (system messages anywhere, developer role, tool calls and results, images, parts of other kinds, odd key orders, empty messages) for five formats, with and without auto caching, with the request arriving a byte at a time or whole and with a 16-byte string limit: the JSON is equal (keys sorted). **What is held**: the small top-level fields, system messages (they are hoisted: Anthropic's `system`, Gemini's `systemInstruction`, Converse's `system`, written after the messages, so system messages anywhere in the list work with no look-ahead; the key order of Anthropic's body is therefore messages, system, parameters, where the whole-body converter writes system first, and Gemini, Converse and Cohere bodies are byte-identical for a request with its system first), tool call arguments, and, when the order is not the usual one, one message or one content part. A message streams when its `role` (and a tool message's `tool_call_id`) comes before its `content`, and a text or image part when its `type` comes before its data; otherwise that message (or part) is held whole and replayed in order. Anything held is bounded by client.max_request_body_size_mb, past which the request is refused with a 400 that names what was too large and how to order the JSON. Turn merging, Anthropic's auto caching (the newest block is closed last so it can take `cache_control`), Converse's cache points, Gemini's function names for tool results and Cohere's in-place system messages follow the whole-body converters. The converted body goes out chunked (its length is not known up front) except for Bedrock. **Bedrock** signs the SHA-256 of the payload (SigV4), and its runtime API takes neither a streaming signature nor an unsigned payload (those are S3's; Bifrost does not stream large payloads to Bedrock either), so the converted bytes are written to segment files in `$TMPDIR` (default /tmp; a directory `raiju-spool-*`, removed when the request ends, stale ones older than 6 hours removed when the next one starts) with an incremental SHA-256 (`crypto.tov` `Sha256`), then signed (`signHashed`) and sent with their length: the gateway's memory stays flat and its disk holds the converted body, up to max_payload_bytes. Because that happens before the request is authorised, a gateway that enforces virtual keys refuses a Bedrock request without one first. All keys of the Bedrock provider must resolve the model to the same id (a key alias that differs sends the request to the buffered path), since the reasoning fields depend on it. **The answer**: a non-streamed answer is small and goes through the ordinary translation and accounting (budgets, token limits, metrics, request log). A chat request that asks for a stream (the flag must lie in the first prefetch_size_bytes, as for the other large routes) is sent to Anthropic, Gemini, Cohere and Vertex as a stream request and the events are converted to chat chunks as they arrive by the usual stream converters (usage from the stream, as for any streamed answer; the chunk with usage follows the caller's `stream_options.include_usage`, read from the body once it is in); for Bedrock, ConverseStream is called and its binary event-stream frames are converted to chat chunks as they arrive (L162; the spooled body is signed as for Converse). The Messages route returns the provider's own Anthropic-format answer as it came (a Vertex Claude stream is passed through and its usage read from its two ends, as L129). Not covered: a body whose big strings are in fields other than message text, image URLs and tool calls is held up to the limit; `prompt_cache.cache_control_injection_points` (buffered, above); injected tools, MCP tools, compat shaping and guardrails are skipped for a large request as for the other large routes; a body is not retried or sent to a fallback provider of another format (400); Anthropic's input `cache_control` blocks inside message content are not carried (as in the whole-body converter); chunked request bodies need a provider or proxy that accepts them (HTTP/2 uses DATA frames). **Multipart uploads** (audio transcriptions and translations, image edits and variations, file uploads; `handleMediaLarge` in `media.tov`): a body over the threshold is read for its first prefetch bytes, `scanMultipart` (largepayload.tov) reads the form fields that lie whole before the file part (model, stream), the model's value is rewritten to the provider's name in place (Bifrost does the same byte rewrite of the model string in the multipart prefix), the length is declared again, and the rest streams to OpenAI-compatible and Azure providers; file uploads (no model) go to the x-bf-provider provider. A form whose file part comes before the model field cannot be routed from the prefix: it is read whole up to client.max_request_body_size_mb and served by the ordinary route (a 413 past it); the same for native providers (Anthropic, Gemini, Vertex, Bedrock, Replicate files). The answer of an upload is read whole (these answers are small) and sent as the provider gave it; the media routes keep no usage, budget or request-log accounting, large or not. **Usage of streamed bodies**: an answer that is passed through (a stream, or an answer over response_threshold_bytes, for a large request or an ordinary one) is watched by `UsageTap`: its first 16 KiB and last 64 KiB are kept (an Anthropic stream reports the prompt in `message_start` and the output in `message_delta`; OpenAI's usage chunk and a Responses `response.completed` event and every JSON answer end with usage), the `"usage":{...}` objects in them are read (`usageOfBytes`: `prompt_tokens`/`completion_tokens`, or `input_tokens`/`output_tokens` with Anthropic's cache tokens counted as prompt, and OpenRouter's `usage.cost` as the billed cost), and when the answer ends the tokens and cost are debited from the key's budgets and token limits, counted in the usage metrics and written to the request log row (`ReqCtx.settle`), the same as an answer held whole. The body is never held. A usage object further than 64 KiB from the end (or a provider whose usage is elsewhere) reads as 0. Open (L146): a Messages request's system prompt written after its messages stays where it stands (the whole-body converter writes it first; moving it ahead would need the messages already sent, so it is left as DESIGN); a message whose content comes before its role is held whole. In a message whose text or images come before its tool results, the results now go first as the whole-body converter writes them (L178: the message's parts are held up to 64 KiB, `msgconv.tov` PART_HOLD, while its tool results go out; parts over that size stream in body order and a tool result after them starts a new chat message). L162: a Messages stream to Bedrock goes to ConverseStream through chat, as the whole-body route sends it (`largeconv.tov` family `messages-converse`: `MsgToChat` chained into the Converse converter, spooled, hashed and signed), and its event-stream frames become Messages events as they arrive; a large chat stream to Bedrock calls ConverseStream the same way. A large `/v1/completions` request that compat's `convert_text_to_chat` sends as chat (the model's datasheet row serves chat but not text completions; the row is read before the body) becomes the chat body as it streams in (`textconv.tov` `TextToChat`: the body `textToChatFields` writes, a prompt list joined with newlines, the kept parameters held and written last) and goes to an OpenAI-format provider's chat endpoint or through the chat converter of Anthropic, Gemini, Vertex, Cohere or Bedrock (families `text-*`); it is answered as a text completion, whole or as `text_completion` chunks converted as they arrive. A large Titan embeddings request becomes one InvokeModel body per input (`embconv.tov` `EMB_TITAN`, `pushTitan`/`finishTitan`; dimensions and normalize end every body whatever the request's order), each spooled to its own files, hashed and signed (`largeconv.tov` `spoolTitan`, `titanExtras`), sent as consecutive calls whose answers are joined as the whole-body route joins them. L178: compat's drops and fits apply to a large text completion: one sent as chat gets them on the parameters `TextToChat` holds, one that goes to an OpenAI-format provider as it is is written again as it streams in when the model's datasheet row can drop or fit something (`CompatPlan.mayShape`; `textconv.tov` `TextShaper`: the prompt, of any JSON shape, copied as it comes, the other members held up to client.max_request_body_size_mb and written after it through `CompatPlan.shapeParams`, with the usage chunk asked for a stream), with the compat headers and warnings of the whole-body route. A large text completion to Replicate becomes its prediction body as it streams in (`TextToChat.forReplicate`: `{"version"?,"input":{"prompt",max_tokens,temperature,top_p,presence_penalty,frequency_penalty,seed}}`, what `replicate.chatBody` writes for the chat request `textToChatFields` gives; family `text-replicate`), sent to the prediction URL with `Prefer: wait`, polled like any Replicate prediction, and answered as a text completion (whole, or replayed as text_completion chunks). e2e `l178_e2e`. **Multipart uploads** (audio transcriptions and translations, image edits and variations, file uploads; `handleMediaLarge` in `media.tov`): a body over the threshold is read for its first prefetch bytes, `scanMultipart` (largepayload.tov) reads the form fields that lie whole before the file part (model, stream), the model's value is rewritten to the provider's name in place (Bifrost does the same byte rewrite of the model string in the multipart prefix), the length is declared again, and the rest streams to OpenAI-compatible and Azure providers; file uploads (no model) go to the x-bf-provider provider. A form whose file part comes before the model field cannot be routed from the prefix: it is read whole up to client.max_request_body_size_mb and served by the ordinary route (a 413 past it); the same for native providers (Anthropic, Gemini, Vertex, Bedrock, Replicate files). The answer of an upload is read whole (these answers are small) and sent as the provider gave it; the media routes keep no usage, budget or request-log accounting, large or not. **Usage of streamed bodies**: an answer that is passed through (a stream, or an answer over response_threshold_bytes, for a large request or an ordinary one) is watched by `UsageTap`: its first 16 KiB and last 64 KiB are kept (an Anthropic stream reports the prompt in `message_start` and the output in `message_delta`; OpenAI's usage chunk and a Responses `response.completed` event and every JSON answer end with usage), the `"usage":{...}` objects in them are read (`usageOfBytes`: `prompt_tokens`/`completion_tokens`, or `input_tokens`/`output_tokens` with Anthropic's cache tokens counted as prompt, and OpenRouter's `usage.cost` as the billed cost), and when the answer ends the tokens and cost are debited from the key's budgets and token limits, counted in the usage metrics and written to the request log row (`ReqCtx.settle`), the same as an answer held whole. The body is never held. A usage object further than 64 KiB from the end (or a provider whose usage is elsewhere) reads as 0. L172: a streamed chat or text completion that goes to an OpenAI-format provider as it is asks for its usage chunk, as Bifrost asks every OpenAI-format stream: `"stream_options":{"include_usage":true},` goes in front of the caller's members inside the prefix, unless the prefix holds a `stream_options` (a later one in the body still wins), and the declared length grows by its size (`largepayload.tov` `LargeBody.askUsage`, `inserted`), so budgets and the log count the usage of every such stream (e2e `l124_e2e`). The body limit and the stream threshold follow the config as it is when a request comes, so a change through the API or a reload moves them on both servers (`server.tov` `bodyLimits`; the http1 server reads the threshold at each new connection; e2e `l172_e2e`); with the feature off a body over client.max_request_body_size_mb now reaches the gateway as a stream and is refused unread. L178: a `"stream": true` after the prefetch is read from the answer: a large request answered with an event stream is labelled a stream (the metrics' method label, the log row's stream flag, `x-bifrost-request-type`); `openai_config.disable_store` ends a large chat or Responses body that goes as it is with `"store":false` (`LargeBody.noStore`: the last bytes are held back until the body ends and the member goes in before the closing brace, so it outvotes a `store` the caller wrote anywhere, and the declared length grows by its size), and so do the chat bodies converted for an OpenAI-format provider (`AnyConv.noStore`), as the whole-body requests force store false. Open: Bun.serve's own stream threshold is the one at start, so a request threshold lowered later applies to bodies over the start one only (smaller ones arrive whole and take the ordinary path, within client.max_request_body_size_mb); a TLS HTTP/1.1 provider that answers early and resets the connection may cost the 413 (HTTP/2 and plain HTTP/1.1 keep it); compat's drops on a large chat, Responses or Messages request are not applied (only a text completion's), as compat shaping is skipped for them; a Messages system prompt after its messages (above). P2. |
| scim_config | config.schema.json scim_config | done | `config_compat.tov` (`mapScim`) keeps `enabled` and `config.provisioningToken` (`env.` allowed), which `scim.tov` and the `/scim/v2` routes read (L115). `authProxy` maps to `auth_proxy` (G45). L181: `provider` and the claim settings (`claimsSyncMode`, `roleResolutionStrategy`, `rolesField`, `teamIdsField`, `userIdField`, and the role, team, business unit, access profile and project attribute mappings) are kept and drive what a sign-in changes (`claimsync.tov`, see (f) SCIM and user provisioning). The provider's sign-in settings become the `oidc` section when the config has none (`config_compat.tov` scimIssuer, scimOidc): Okta's and the generic provider's issuerUrl, Entra's tenant on its cloud's login host (commercial, or login.microsoftonline.us for gcc-high and dod), Keycloak's realm under its serverUrl, Zitadel's domain, Google's accounts host; clientId and clientSecret (env. references read at start, `ops.tov` configureOidc); the generic provider's scopes, Okta's additionalScopes added to the defaults; the redirect URI the gateway's own /api/oidc/callback on the host the sign-in came to (`ops.tov` withRedirect). An `oidc` section of the config's own wins (noted). `trusted_networks` is kept and changes nothing: Raiju's identity-provider discovery calls have no private-address guard to bypass (noted). L191: the provider's own settings are kept as well (`config_compat.tov` SCIM_KEPT_KEYS) for directory sync and bulk import (`directory.tov` directorySettings): Okta's apiToken on the issuer's host; Entra's tenantId, clientId and clientSecret on Microsoft Graph for its cloud (graph.microsoft.us for gcc-high, dod-graph.microsoft.us for dod); Keycloak's serverUrl, realm and the client's service account; Zitadel's domain, projectId and serviceAccountClientId/serviceAccountClientSecret; Google's domain, adminEmail and serviceAccountJson, or credentialMode env (serviceAccountEnvVar) or file (serviceAccountFile). `enableBulkSync` false turns directory access off; `bulkSyncInterval` is whole days or h/m/s pairs from 1h to 30d (24h by default; a value out of range is clamped and noted, `config_compat.tov` bulkSyncInterval) and a changed one applies without a restart; `claimScimAttributes` is read by `claimsync.tov` scimClaims. Raiju's own `directoryUrl` and `directoryTokenUrl` replace the directory API's base URL and token endpoint (a proxy, or a test double; the providers' next-page links are moved onto it). `audience` becomes the oidc section's `audiences` (an ID token must carry one of them; the client id when unset) and Entra's `appIdUri` is added to them for v1.0 tokens (`oidc.tov` acceptedAudiences; a wrong one fails with "invalid audience"). Keycloak's sign-in reads the access token's claims (`claims_token` "access_token", `oidc.tov` accessTokenClaims: signature, issuer and lifetime checked, and an accepted audience or the client as azp). The derived oidc section asks for Bifrost's scopes, openid profile email offline_access (Google's without offline_access, which it refuses), so a sign-in carries the refresh token a session refresh uses. The provider's secrets (clientSecret, apiToken, serviceAccountClientSecret, serviceAccountJson) are masked in GET /api/config. e2e `l191_e2e`. EXTERNAL: credentialMode inherit (Google's Application Default Credentials) and impersonateServiceAccount are kept and noted, and directory access stays off without a key; SailPoint has no OIDC sign-in or directory API here (noted; its SCIM pushes work); checks against a real identity provider. |
| access_profiles (top-level) | config.schema.json access_profiles | partial | Read as `governance.access_profiles` (`config_compat.tov` mapAccessProfiles; L112a). A profile's name becomes its id (lower-cased, other characters as "-"). Per provider: all_models_allowed, allowed_models, blacklisted_models (as profile denies), weight, one budget and one rate limit, which join governance under their own ids. L180: virtual_mcps entries by virtual_mcp_id resolve to the virtual MCP with that id in mcp.virtual_mcps (the id wins over a name, as in Bifrost; an unknown id is noted and not granted). Not applied: a second budget on a profile or provider, reset_config, calendar_aligned, allow_all_providers next to provider_configs, key_ids, model_budgets, and the deprecated mcp_tool_groups, mcp_servers and mcp_tool_overrides. When `governance.access_profiles` is also set, the top-level list is ignored. |
| feature_flags | config.schema.json feature_flags | done | `config.tov` FeatureFlagsConfig, boot-time overrides of the flags in `flags.tov` (L88). |
| proxy_config (global outbound proxy) | config.schema.json proxy_config | done | `config.tov` GlobalProxyConfig; GET and PUT on `/api/proxy-config`. Used by a provider with no proxy of its own when `enable_for_inference` is set. Bifrost's schema allows only type `http`; other types give no proxy. Startup warning bug: see LIMITS. |
| framework.pricing (pricing_url, pricing_sync_interval) | config.schema.json framework; framework/modelcatalog/config.go:109 (default URL) | done | `pricing_url` and `pricing_sync_interval` become `pricing_sync` (`config_compat.tov` mapPricing). Sync runs only when a pricing section is set. `mcp_library_url` and `mcp_library_sync_interval` become `pricing_sync.mcp_library_url` and `.mcp_library_sync_interval` (the MCP library sync, L166). L180: `model_parameters_url` becomes `pricing_sync.model_parameters_url`: GET /api/models/parameters (and the compat plugin) read the model parameters datasheet from it (http, https or file://; default the pricing URL's /model-parameters), read again when it changes. `live_models_sync_interval` becomes `pricing_sync.live_models_sync_interval` (`model_list.tov` liveModelsSeconds: unset is Bifrost's hour, 0 turns the refresher off, a value under 60 s is raised to 60 with a note): every serving process re-lists each provider key's models in the background at that interval, jittered by 10 percent (`server.tov` startLiveModelRefresher, Bifrost's startLiveModelRefresher), and a listing is served for the interval (until refreshed on demand when the refresher is off); an edited key is listed again, and a failed read keeps the last good listing for five minutes. PUT /api/config takes both in framework_config, with Bifrost's checks (an empty model_parameters_url resets it; a live interval is 0 or at least 60 seconds). |
| Raiju-only top-level keys (cache, prompt_cache, health, pricing, otel, semantic_cache, session_affinity, complexity_router, mcp_server, virtual_mcps, log_exports, exporters, cluster, oidc, alerts, audit, secrets, guardrails, circuit_breaker_policies, prompt_repo, auth_proxy) | not in Bifrost (several correspond to Bifrost sections above) | done (Raiju-only) | `config.tov` Config type. Bifrost configs that use the Bifrost names for these will not load into the Raiju fields. |

### c2. client.*

Bifrost: `transports/config.schema.json` `$defs/client` (and `client.*` properties). Raiju: `ClientConfig` in `src/config.tov`, the mapping in `src/config_compat.tov` (`mapClient`, `mapGovernancePlugin`, `mapLogging`), and the consumers named below.

| Feature | Bifrost | Raiju status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| allowed_origins | client.allowed_origins | done | `server.tov` cors. |
| enable_logging | client.enable_logging | done | `config.tov` ClientConfig; read in `gateway.tov`. |
| enforce_governance_header | client.enforce_governance_header | done | `governance.tov` govConfig. Gates the VK requirement when enforce_auth_on_inference is unset (its older name in Bifrost), default false. |
| enforce_auth_on_inference | client.enforce_auth_on_inference | done | Read as the gate (`governance.tov` govConfig, commit 32da2e6). When unset, it is on when any credentials exist. |
| required_headers | client.required_headers | done | `governance.tov` govConfig. |
| max_request_body_size_mb | client.max_request_body_size_mb | done | `main.tov` passes it to both servers: http1 (`startFront`) and Bun (`server.tov` limitedBody: a declared length or the read body over the limit gets a 413; L97g). |
| async_job_result_ttl | client.async_job_result_ttl | done | `server.tov` dispatch (the `/v1/async/*` TTL). The per-request header is missing (see b1). |
| mcp_agent_depth | client.mcp_agent_depth | done | `gateway.tov` Engine constructor; `agent.tov` (the depth limit for the tool loop). |
| mcp_tool_execution_timeout | client.mcp_tool_execution_timeout | done | Mapped to `mcp.tool_execution_timeout_seconds` (`config_compat.tov` mapClient), the global timeout. A client's own `tool_execution_timeout` (a Go duration or a number of seconds, in config.json and the management API) overrides it for that client's calls (`mcp.tov` McpSession constructor, `mcpapi.tov` timeoutFrom); verified in L166, the earlier note was stale. |
| mcp_tool_sync_interval | client.mcp_tool_sync_interval | done | Read as `mcp.tool_sync_interval` (`config_compat.tov` mapClient), the global default (c5). |
| mcp_auto_execute | not in Bifrost (Bifrost: per-client tools_to_auto_execute) | done (Raiju-only) | `gateway.tov` withMcpTools and chat. Per-client `tools_to_auto_execute` is in c5. |
| disable_content_logging | client.disable_content_logging | done | `gateway.tov` contentOff (commit 12757de). |

Already built; L112a confirmed it and removed the stale "not implemented" report. |
| delete_expired_virtual_keys, hide_deleted_virtual_keys_in_filters, enforce_vk_object_ownership, hidden_request_types | client.* | done | delete_expired_virtual_keys is done (L97j): `govapi.tov` `expiredToDelete` and `store.tov` `sweepExpiredKeys`, run hourly by `ops.tov` `startExpirySweep` (one process only, the supervisor or a single-worker server, since a worker's config copy may be behind the file); a key's own delete_after_expire overrides it. hide_deleted_virtual_keys_in_filters is accepted with no effect: Raiju deletes keys outright, so none are hidden. enforce_vk_object_ownership (L179; on by default, as in Bifrost) binds provider files and batches to the virtual key that created them (`objowner.tov`, wired in `pluginroute.tov` `storageHooked`, so the OpenAI, Anthropic, GenAI and native Bedrock/Vertex file and batch routes all apply it): the create answer records the key (one small file per object in `<logs_store.dir>/provider-objects`, shared by a node's workers and kept across restarts; memory only without a logs store); a retrieve, content, cancel, results or delete call by another key is refused with Bifrost's 404 `access_blocked` "file (or batch) '<id>' not found" before the provider is called; a batch create on another key's input file (input_file_id, or a Gemini src/input_config file_name) is refused the same way; list pages (data, files, batches, invocationJobSummaries) leave other keys' objects out, cursors untouched; a batch retrieve binds its output and error files to the batch's creator; a confirmed delete forgets the record. A request with no virtual key, and an object with no record, stay unrestricted, and creators are recorded while the option is off, as in Bifrost. Ids are matched in their Gemini short and long forms and by a Bedrock job ARN's trailing id; records are keyed by kind and id, not provider (Bifrost keys them per provider and checks across equivalent provider names). e2e `l179_e2e`. hidden_request_types is applied (L168) as Bifrost's withHiddenRequestTypes: the request log reads (list, stats, one log, sessions, histograms, rankings, dashboard, filterdata) leave out the LLM entries of those types (`ops.tov` `withoutHidden`), by Bifrost's request type names (chat_completion, chat_completion_stream, embedding, transcription, ...; `logstore.tov` `bifrostRequestType`, with the entry's `streamed` flag) or Raiju's op names; they are still stored, deleted and repriced. L193 closes the rest of enforce_vk_object_ownership, as Bifrost's providerjobs.go: Gemini cached contents (native GenAI and Vertex routes) are bound to their creator (retrieve, update and delete by another key are 404 access_blocked "cached content '<name>' not found", lists narrowed, a delete forgets); a cached content create or a batch create that names another key's file (fileUri, input_file_id, fileName, inline items) is refused; an inference request that names another key's object is refused before any route reads it (`server.tov` `ownedInference`, `pluginroute.tov` `ownedRequest`: chat, Responses, Messages and GenAI generation, streamed or not, a file by file_id or file_ids or a Gemini file URI in any URL, a cached content by cached_content or cachedContent; `objowner.tov` `objectRefs`); a passthrough call's path (files/<id>, batches/<id>, cachedContents/<id>) and body are checked the same way and a passthrough GET of a collection is narrowed (`passthroughOwned`); the GenAI SDK's resumable files.upload records its creator (`recordUpload`); and records carry the provider they were made through, holding for that provider and any provider that reaches the same upstream (a custom provider and its base, or two on one base, with the same base URL; `sameUpstream`), as Bifrost's equivalentProviders (records from before hold for every provider). The body check runs only for a request with a virtual key while some object is recorded. Containers and videos are out of scope (videos and containers stay Out). e2e `l193_e2e`. |
| routing_chain_max_depth | client.routing_chain_max_depth | done | Bounds key alias chains (`aliases.tov` resolveKeyAlias, `router.tov` resolveAlias; default 10). Bifrost's setting is for routing-rule chains, which Raiju takes as its key aliases (L97g). |
