| routing plugin | plugins/routing (complexity classifiers, decision models) | done | CEL variables and RE2 `matches` (`cel.tov`, `regex.tov`). Complexity classifiers heuristic, decision (decision-model, L81), llm and semantic (`complexity.tov`). chain_rule (L97m): a matched rule with chain_rule re-routes its target through the rules, up to routing_chain_max_depth, stopping on a terminal rule, no match, or no change (`gateway.tov` route, `cel.tov` RouteMatch.chain). |
| semanticcache plugin | plugins/semanticcache/main.go | done | `semcache.tov`, with Qdrant, Redis, Weaviate, Pinecone and chromem through `vectorstore.tov` (commits d91ef07, 0d24b99). cache_tool_call_responses and disable_identity_scoping are applied (L77). Options are read by `config_compat.tov`. |
| circuit-breaker (docs/features/plugins/circuit-breaker.mdx) | docs | done | `health.tov`. Config location differs (c1). |
| Plugin HTTP transport hooks (HTTPTransportPreHook, HTTPTransportPostHook, HTTPTransportStreamChunkHook; WASM `http_pre_hook`, `http_post_hook`, `http_stream_chunk_hook`) | core/schemas/plugin.go; transports/bifrost-http/handlers/middlewares.go (TransportInterceptorMiddleware); lib/config.go (pluginChunkInterceptor); docs/plugins/writing-wasm-plugin.mdx | partial | L130: `pluginroute.tov` `transportStart` (before routing), `transportEnd` (the response) and `transportSink` (each chunk of a stream), called by both servers (`server.tov` `bunHttp`, `chatStreamed`, `hookedDispatch`; `front.tov` `handle`); the plugin side is `plugins.tov` `BasePlugin.httpPre`/`httpPost`/`httpChunk`, `PluginChain.runHttpPre`/`runHttpPost`/`runHttpChunk`, `WasmPlugin` and `wasmplugin.tov` `httpPre`/`httpPost`/`httpChunk`; the request and response JSON is `plughttp.tov`. They run on the inference routes (`plughttp.tov` `isInferencePath`: the OpenAI, Anthropic, GenAI, Bedrock, Cohere and SDK-prefix route families, not the management API, health, metrics, the dashboard, SCIM, A2A, MCP or WebSocket routes, and not a CORS preflight). The pre hook gets `{method, path, headers, query, path_params, body}` (headers lower-cased, the body base64, a repeated query parameter as its last value) and answers `{context, request, response, has_response, error}`: headers and query parameters it sets replace the caller's (Bifrost applies only sets, and so does Raiju), a body that differs replaces the body, `has_response` answers the request at once (status_code, headers, body; the later plugins and the provider are skipped), a non-empty `error` fails the request with 500, and a plugin that changes the method or path is refused with 409 as Bifrost refuses it. The post hook runs in reverse after the response, gets `{context, request, response}` and answers `{context, error}` (and, as an extension, `response` with status_code, headers and body to rewrite it; the compiled-in plugins change the response in place); an error message stops the chain and keeps what was changed so far. It is not called for a streamed answer (as in Bifrost), nor for a body that is already a stream (a large answer); an event stream that is built whole goes through the chunk hook instead. The chunk hook runs in reverse for each event of a streamed answer on both servers (the event's JSON, no SSE framing; `[DONE]`, comments and non-JSON data pass): `skip` drops the chunk, a chunk is sent on (changed or not), an error with no chunk ends the stream with `data: {"error":"<message>"}` and no `[DONE]` and stops reading the upstream, an error with a chunk is logged. A hook that traps, runs out of its limits or answers with bad JSON is logged and skipped. The plugin context travels from `http_pre_hook` to `pre_hook`, `post_hook` and `http_post_hook` (`Engine.pluginRuns`, found by the `x-bf-plugin-run` header the transport hooks put on the request; the header is the gateway's own and never goes upstream). Compiled-in plugins have the same three hooks; the `header` plugin's `set_response_headers` (Raiju's own) uses `httpPost`. `/api/plugins` lists `types` llm and http. L170: the hooks run on `/mcp` and `/mcp/{name}` (`plughttp.tov` `isInferencePath`); Bedrock's binary event streams go through the chunk hook frame by frame (`pluginroute.tov` `transportSink`, `frameStep`, `framesWhole`; `streams.tov` `EventFrames`): the plugin sees the frame's JSON payload, a changed payload is framed again, `skip` drops the frame and an error ends the stream with an exception frame; a pre-hook error is answered with the bare text (`bareError`, text/plain), as Bifrost sends it; each hook call is a plugin span (`plugin.<name>.transportprehook` and so on) and its output a plugin log (see the LLM hooks row). L176: a request body over 4 MiB (`plughttp.tov` `HOOK_BODY_LIMIT`, declared or read) no longer skips the hooks: they run with an empty body, as Bifrost's fasthttpToHTTPRequest skips a large payload's body; headers and query they set apply, a body they set replaces the request's, and otherwise the body goes on as it came, still streaming when it was not read (so large_payload_optimization takes it); an answer over 4 MiB goes to http_post_hook with an empty body the same way (`pluginroute.tov` `transportStart`, `rebuildStream`, `transportEnd`). Open (DESIGN): Bifrost's `HTTPTransportPreAuthHook` and `HTTPTransportResponseHeadersHook` are not in the WASM ABI and are not built; the chunk is the client-format event (OpenAI, Responses or Anthropic events), not Bifrost's internal chunk, so a plugin sees an Anthropic Messages stream as Anthropic events; events are cut at blank lines (CRLF is normalised to LF). P2. |
| Plugin LLM hooks on every request type and on streams (PreLLMHook, PostLLMHook; WASM `pre_hook`, `post_hook`) | core/bifrost.go (RunLLMPreHooks, RunPostLLMHooks); core/providers/utils/utils.go (ProcessAndSendResponse); docs/plugins/writing-wasm-plugin.mdx | partial | L130: chat runs the chain in `gateway.tov` `chatOnce` as before; embeddings, text completions, Responses, rerank, speech, transcription and translation (multipart), image generation, edit and variation and OCR run it in `pluginroute.tov` `llmHooked` (`server.tov` `routes`, `routeStream` and the async-job `dispatcher`). A WASM plugin gets the request as `{provider, model, input, params, fallbacks, request_type}` flat and under the type's key (`chat_request`, `text_completion_request`, `embedding_request`, `responses_request`, `rerank_request`, `speech_request`, `transcription_request`, `image_generation_request`, `image_edit_request`, `image_variation_request`, `ocr_request`; `plughttp.tov` `LlmKind` has the table): `input` is the body's own input field (messages, prompt, input) and the rest are `params`; a type with no input (rerank, transcription, OCR) has params only; a multipart request is its text fields (the files are not passed) and a change to them is applied to the form; the response is under `<type>_response` (speech audio as `{"audio": base64, "content_type"}`). `pre_hook` can rewrite the request, or short-circuit with a response of the type (`<type>_response`) or an error; `post_hook` runs in reverse for the plugins whose `pre_hook` ran. On a stream `post_hook` runs for each chunk (`gateway.tov` `llmStreamSink`, chat, text completions and Responses streams; the chunk is under `<type>_response`, or `responses_stream_response` for Responses events) and before the HTTP chunk hooks, as in Bifrost. `has_error`: a `post_hook` answer with `has_error` and a non-empty `error` turns the response into that error (`status_code` 400 to 599, else 500; the body is `{"error": ...}`), on whole answers and on a chunk (the stream ends with the error as its last event, no `[DONE]`); a provider error reaches `post_hook` as `{response: null, error: {status_code, error: {message, type, ...}}, has_error: true}` and a plugin may recover from it (an empty error with a `response`, as Bifrost reads an empty error) or change it; for a chat answer the error replaces the response inside the attempt, so the next fallback is tried (Bifrost allows fallbacks when `AllowFallbacks` is nil). The compiled-in plugins (mocker, jsonparser, blocklist, maxtokens, header) run on chat only (`BasePlugin.handlesOp`), as Bifrost's mocker and jsonparser run on chat and Responses only; L159 puts them on the provider-format chat routes (below). L139: the Anthropic Messages (`/anthropic/v1/messages`, and `/anthropic/v1/complete` through it), Cohere chat, GenAI `generateContent` and `streamGenerateContent`, and Bedrock `converse`, `converse-stream`, `invoke` and `invoke-with-response-stream` routes run the hooks when a plugin opts in (`BasePlugin.hooksNative`; WASM plugins do; since L159 a compiled-in one does for a request it would act on): `nativehook.tov` `viaChat` converts the request to the chat request (`anthropic_in`, `cohere_in`, `genai_in`, `bedrock_in` converters), serves it through `engine.chat` where `pre_hook`, `post_hook` and the per-chunk `post_hook` run as for any chat request, and converts the answer back (`responseToNative`; `NativeStream` for the chunks, Bedrock as event-stream frames). A plugin's rewrite of the request is what the provider gets; a short circuit (a chat response) is returned in the route's own format, and a stream request gets it as a stream; a `has_error` answer or a short-circuit error keeps the chat route's error body and status, and on a stream ends it with the format's error (an `error` event, or a Bedrock exception frame). With such a plugin loaded these routes are served through chat: an Anthropic, Gemini or Bedrock target gets the converted chat request, not the native body, and the Messages-only fields that chat does not carry are lost. Cohere embed and rerank, GenAI `:predict` embeddings and `rank`, and Cursor's Responses-shaped chat call `pluginroute.tov` `llmHooked` on the internal request (`native.tov` `hooked`). L159: the compiled-in plugins take part on the Anthropic Messages, Cohere chat, GenAI generate and Bedrock Converse/Invoke routes where they would act on the request (`BasePlugin.appliesNative`, `PluginChain.hooksNativeFor`, called by `nativehook.tov` `hooksOn` with the converted chat request): blocklist when a blocked word is in it, maxtokens when it would set or lower max_tokens, header when it has set_request_headers, mocker when a rule matches or default_behavior answers, jsonparser when strip_markdown_fences is on; such a request is served through chat as above, and a request none of them acts on keeps the route's own path (an Anthropic or Bedrock target gets the native body). GenAI `:embedContent` and `:batchEmbedContents` (with a plugin in the embedding hooks they are served through the embeddings route, Gemini included, and answered in Gemini's shape), `/bedrock/rerank` (the rerank request, answered in Bedrock's shape), the count-tokens routes (`/v1/count_tokens`, `/anthropic/v1/messages/count_tokens`, GenAI `:countTokens`, Bedrock `count-tokens`; request type `count_tokens`, `count_tokens_request`/`count_tokens_response`, the input being the body's `messages`, Bedrock's `input` or GenAI's `contents`; a route whose body names no model shows the plugins the route's model and drops it again before counting) and the batch creations (Anthropic `POST /anthropic/v1/messages/batches`, GenAI `batches.create`, Bedrock `POST /bedrock/model-invocation-job`; request type `batch_create`, the body as params) run the LLM hooks through `llmHooked` (`native.tov` `modelHooked`, `extras.tov` `countTokens`, `bedrockRerank`, `server.tov`). Without a plugin that handles the type these routes serve as before. L170: the batch and file calls run the LLM hooks as Bifrost's do (`pluginroute.tov` `storageHooked`, `StorageCall`; `plughttp.tov` kinds `batch_list`, `batch_retrieve`, `batch_cancel`, `batch_results`, `batch_delete`, `file_upload`, `file_list`, `file_retrieve`, `file_delete`, `file_content`): the OpenAI `/v1/batches` and `/v1/files` routes, the Anthropic SDK batch and file routes, GenAI batches and files, Bedrock model-invocation jobs and S3 files, and Vertex batch jobs (`server.tov` `nativeStorage`); the request is `{provider, <id key>, ...query}` under `<kind>_request` (an upload is its form fields, not the file), a changed id or provider is the one called, a short circuit answers in the route's format, and `post_hook` gets the answer (file content as `{file_id, content_type}`). `pre_hook` runs again on each fallback attempt of a chat request, on the request as it came, after the failed attempt's `post_hook` (`gateway.tov` `AttemptPlugins`), and an error short circuit moves on to the next fallback unless it sets `allow_fallbacks: false`; a cache hit (exact or semantic) runs `post_hook` for the plugins placed before the cache (pre_builtin and builtin, as the cache is a built-in plugin in Bifrost), and an answer they change is not stored; a `post_hook` answer `{"stream_control": {"skip_stream": true}}` (WASM) or `{kind: "skip"}` drops the chunk (Bifrost's StreamControl); every hook call is a plugin span (`plugin.<name>.prehook`, `.posthook` accumulated over a stream's chunks; `plugins.tov` `PluginTrace`, `timed`) exported with the request's trace, and a plugin's printed output and hook errors are its plugin logs (the log entry's `plugin_logs`, a JSON string grouped by plugin as Bifrost stores it). L176: the Responses lifecycle calls (`GET`, `DELETE /v1/responses/{id}`, `POST .../cancel`, `GET .../input_items`; request types `responses_retrieve`, `responses_delete`, `responses_cancel`, `responses_input_items`, the request `{provider, response_id, ...query}`) and the cached contents, GenAI's `/genai/v1beta/cachedContents[/{id}]` and Vertex's regional `/genai/v1beta/projects/{p}/locations/{l}/cachedContents[/{id}]` (`cached_content_create`, `_list`, `_retrieve`, `_update`, `_delete`; the id as `name`, a creation's body with its model as `provider/model`, which goes back into the body) run the LLM hooks as the batch and file calls do (`pluginroute.tov` `storageOp`, `storageIdKey`, `storageHooked`; `server.tov` `nativeStorage`): a changed id is the one called, a short circuit answers, post_hook gets the answer, and a stored response replayed as a stream (`?stream=true`) goes on as it streams. The post_builtin plugins' pre hooks run after the cache lookup, at the primary attempt (`PluginChain.runPreBeforeCache`, `runPreAfterCache`; `gateway.tov` `AttemptPlugins.afterCache`), as Bifrost runs them after its builtin plugins: a cache hit never reaches them, their changes do not enter the cache key, and the gateway's own checks (governance, budgets) come first; their short circuit answers the attempt (or, an error, moves on to the fallbacks unless allow_fallbacks is false). An error event in a stream (`data: {"error": ...}`) goes through the chunk post hooks as an error (`PluginChain.runPostChunkError`, status 500): a plugin may change it, drop it, or recover with a chunk sent in its place. The spans of the post hooks that run after a non-chat route's handler wrote its log row are exported with the request's trace: the trace waits for them (`PluginTrace.defer`, `onDone`, `done`; `pluginroute.tov` `llmHooked`). The pre hooks run again on each fallback of text completions, embeddings, Responses and Cohere and vLLM rerank too (`pluginroute.tov` hookedRun registers the request's AttemptPlugins, which serve finds by x-bf-plugin-attempt; the builds read the attempt's body), after the failed attempt's post hooks, as on chat. Open: GPT Live billing units do not run the hooks (Bifrost runs them per unit with an empty Responses request); per-fallback `pre_hook` does not run on the multipart routes or Bedrock rerank; `PreRequestHook` is not a separate phase (DESIGN: the WebAssembly ABI has no such hook and Go .so plugins are owner decision 1, and the phase's Bifrost users, routing, governance and the model catalog resolver, are built into the router here). | header plugin (set_request_headers, set_response_headers) | not in Bifrost | done (Raiju-only) | `plugins.tov` (`header`). `set_request_headers` go upstream with chat requests; `set_response_headers` (L130) are set on every answer of an inference route by the HTTP post hook, not on a stream. |

| Dynamic plugins (Go shared objects, WASM) | docs/plugins/getting-started.mdx, writing-go-plugin.mdx, writing-wasm-plugin.mdx | done (owner decision: WASM only, Go .so impossible) | WASM done for the LLM hooks (L121): a `plugins[]` entry whose `path` is a local `.wasm` file is loaded by `wasmplugin.tov` `openWasmPlugin` and runs in the chain as `plugins.tov` `WasmPlugin`, with Bifrost's JSON ABI (exports malloc, free, get_name, init, pre_hook, post_hook, cleanup; packed u64 results; the request as `{provider, model, input, params, fallbacks}` flat and under `chat_request`, the response as `{chat_response}`; the shared `context` between hooks). pre_hook can rewrite the request or short-circuit with a chat response or an error with its status; post_hook can rewrite the response. A hook that traps, returns `error`/`hook_error`, runs out of its limits or answers with bad JSON is logged and skipped, as Bifrost's pipeline skips a failed hook, and a trapped instance is replaced by a fresh one (init runs again). The engine is WAMR (interpreter) added to the Tov runtime by `tov-patches/0005-wamr.patch` (`std/wasm`), no JavaScriptCore; limits per plugin in `wasm_limits` on the entry (`max_memory_pages` 1024, `max_stack_bytes` 262144, `max_instructions` 200000000 per hook call). The plugin sees only its memory and a WASI subset (stdout/stderr captured and logged, clock, random). `/api/plugins` takes `path` and `wasm_limits`, answers 400 for a `.so`, a missing file, a module that does not instantiate or whose init fails. Go shared objects (`.so`) stay unsupported: Bifrost loads them with Go's `plugin` package (`framework/plugins/soloader.go`), which needs the Go runtime, and Tov's only dlopen (`runtime/napi.c` `__tov_dlopen`) loads Node-API addons. L130: the HTTP transport hooks (`http_pre_hook`, `http_post_hook`, `http_stream_chunk_hook`; `http_intercept`, the older name of `http_pre_hook`, is read when that is all the module exports) are called, the LLM hooks run on every request type and on each chunk of a stream, and a `post_hook` answer with `has_error` turns the response into an error (see the next two rows). L170: a `path` that is an http or https URL is downloaded as Bifrost's `DownloadPlugin` does (`plugindl.tov`: 120 s, at most 5 redirects each to http or https, a 200 only, at most 200 MiB, no private, loopback, link-local or metadata address unless the host is in `server.plugin_download_private_allowlist`), at start (`main.tov`), on a reload and on `/api/plugins`, and kept under `RAIJU_PLUGIN_DIR` by the URL's hash; L186 (stricter than Bifrost): the URL and each redirect must be https unless the entry pins the file with `sha256` (64 hex digits, a Raiju key), which is checked on the download and each time the copy (or a pinned local file) is loaded; each hop's host is resolved and every address checked (`netguard.tov`); the cache folder defaults to `plugin-cache` beside the config file (`$TMPDIR/raiju-plugins` when that folder is read-only), is made 0700, and a copy in a folder others can write to is not loaded; a plugin whose download failed at start or on a reload is in error ("has not been downloaded"), the download error being logged. Each hook call also has a wall-clock bound (`wasm_limits.max_time_ms`, default 5000; `tov-patches/0010-wasm-time-limit.patch`, checked by the interpreter every 65536 instructions), so a slow hook is stopped and skipped like one out of instructions. Design (owner decision 1 after wave 14): Go shared objects stay unsupported (above). Tested in `tests/wasm/plugin.c` (compiled to `plugin.wasm`; `make wasm-fixture`), `wasmplugin.tov`, `plugins.tov` and `tests/e2e.py` `l121_e2e`. P2. L188: the runtime checks the addresses again when each connection is made (fetch's `peer` option, `tov-patches/0013-fetch-peer-policy.patch`), so a DNS answer that changes after the check (rebinding) is refused too. |

Raiju's own gateway steps are not plugins, so placement orders only the plugins in the chain. A plugin loaded from a path (WebAssembly) takes part in the same ordering. |

## (f) Governance

Bifrost: `docs/features/governance/*.mdx`, `docs/enterprise/*.mdx`, and `handlers/governance.go`, `virtualkeyrotation.go`, `virtualkeyexpiry.go`, `logvisibility.go`. Raiju: `src/governance.tov`, `src/profiles.tov`, `src/rbac.tov`, `src/pricing.tov`, `src/guardrails.tov`, `src/store.tov` (management routes).

A rotation revokes the rotated keys' MCP OAuth grants, as Bifrost does (`store.tov` rotatedKeyIds, `server.tov` revokeSubject("vk", id) after a 200 on a rotate path; `mcpissuer.tov`). |

The cleanup (L97j): `govapi.tov` `expiredToDelete`, `store.tov` `sweepExpiredKeys`, `ops.tov` `startExpirySweep` (hourly rather than Bifrost's daily job; the notification is not published). |

Users hold profiles (`/api/users/{name}/access-profiles`) and link them to their own keys; the first held profile governs `/api/users/me/vk-creation-policy`, Role binding (L103): `POST /api/governance/access-profiles/{id}/roles` (role_ids, is_default, apply_to_existing; the reply counts the holders) and `DELETE .../roles/{role_id}`. Groups are teams. A pushed user takes a role from `roles` when it names a known one, else `viewer`. L181: claim mapping at sign-in when the identity provider is configured through scim_config (a provider set, and no role_map or default_role in the oidc section; `claimsync.tov`, `ops.tov` syncSignIn), as Bifrost's how-identity-sync-works describes it: the user is found by subject or email or created (named by its address), its role decided from the attribute mappings for roles (dot-path claims, values without regard to case, `*` and globs; several matches resolved by roleResolutionStrategy, the most permissions by default or list order) or with none from the roles claim (rolesField) naming a role; there is no default role: a sign-in that resolves none is refused, the role taken away when the claims say so (a mapped attribute with no matching value, a mapped role that does not exist) and kept when they say nothing; teams (the team mappings, `*` passing each value through, or the teamIdsField claim), business units (created when missing), projects (never created) and access profiles (by name) are resynced from their mappings, memberships an admin added kept apart from the ones claims gave (`synced`), the last value of each claim remembered so a token that leaves one out changes nothing. With SCIM provisioning on (a token set) and claimsSyncMode scim, a user SCIM has not pushed is refused ("Provisioning is managed by SCIM") and a pushed one signs in with the role SCIM gave. POST /api/scim/provisioning-token rotates the provisioning token (returned once; admin only). e2e `l181_scim_e2e`. L191 (`directory.tov`, `provision.tov`, `claimsync.tov`, `ops.tov` provisionRoute, refreshSession, configureDirectory): directory sync and bulk import read the identity provider's directory API (Okta's Users and Groups API with the apiToken, its Link-header pages; Microsoft Graph with client credentials, its users, group memberships and the app's roles assigned to a user or a group, its @odata.nextLink pages; Keycloak's Admin REST API with the client's service account, each user's groups by path and effective realm roles; Zitadel's user and grant search with a service account, machine users left out; Google's Directory API with a service account's signed assertion for adminEmail, its page tokens, users' primary organization, org unit, custom schemas and group addresses). Each user carries the attributes its sign-in token would (profile fields, groups, roles), so the same mapping rules decide it. POST /api/scim/provision/preview shows up to 50 users a filter selects (groups, roles, departments, ids) with what an import would do and the groups to filter by; POST /api/scim/provision/import creates or updates them; POST /api/scim/provision/sync (and the bulkSyncInterval schedule on the leader, GET /api/scim/provision/status) re-reads every user, creates or updates the active ones, removes identity-provider users the directory reports inactive and, after a complete listing, the ones it no longer lists once a lookup confirms them inactive or gone. Both answer 202 with an operation_id (GET /api/scim/provision/jobs/{operation_id}, the newest 50 kept) and are refused with 403 while SCIM provisioning is on. Removal takes the user's virtual keys and project memberships and ends its sessions; users an admin made, users with their own credentials and the last enabled admin are never removed. POST /api/scim/provision/reconcile re-decides users from the claims stored at their last sign-in, sync or SCIM push (what SCIM sent, in SCIM-only mode), all or user_ids, with keep_missing; .../reconcile/preview (a sample of 50 changes, who would lose every access profile, counts) and .../reconcile/preview/user show it without saving, and a reconcile sent with a preview's rules_hash under changed rules is refused with 409; manual users and users with no stored data are skipped. Session refresh: the refresh token stays on the server with the session, an expired session is refreshed silently before the management request and its fresh claims applied again (the access token's for Keycloak); a refresh the provider refuses ends the session and removes the user when the directory reports them inactive or gone (not in SCIM-only mode); a daily sweep on the leader refreshes sessions about to expire (skipped with SCIM provisioning on). SCIM pushes keep the attributes they send (flattened: core attributes, complex ones dotted, the enterprise and custom extensions by field name) and the SCIM groups a user is in, and with mapping rules every SCIM create, replace or patch re-decides the role, teams, business units, projects and access profiles from them through claimScimAttributes ("group" claims read the SCIM groups' names, "user" claims a renamed attribute, without regard to case); data that maps no role leaves the user with none; SCIM-only sign-ins re-apply the rules to what SCIM sent. A SCIM deactivation removes the user's keys, memberships and profiles and keeps its pushed profile (a reactivation restores what the rules give and its SCIM groups); a delete removes its keys too. A refusal that takes the role away still resyncs the memberships. A refused sign-in raises the "Sign-in refused" notice, at most once an hour per user and reason (`ops.tov` refused). The team member list and the new GET, POST /api/governance/business-units/{id}/users and DELETE .../users/{user_id} tell each member's source (manual, or synced from the identity provider: a mapping rule or a SCIM group), and a synced business-unit membership is not removed by hand (409). e2e `l191_e2e`. Open (DESIGN): ETags (Bifrost's docs do not describe them); the jobs live in memory and do not resume after a restart (Bifrost's durable job table; Raiju keeps no database, owner decision 5); the notice reaches every reader of /api/notifications (Raiju has no per-role notices). EXTERNAL: checks against a real identity provider. OIDC sign-in refuses a user that governance lists as disabled, matched by email or subject, and an open session of that user stops at its next request (L120); users governance does not list sign in as before. The identity-aware proxy and credentials refuse a disabled user too. |
| Audit logs (hash chain, HMAC, export, verify) | enterprise/audit-logs.mdx | done | `audit.tov` (`actionFor`, `maskDetail`, chain, export, verify). Retention (L97p): `AuditLog.pruneBefore` deletes events older than `audit.retention_days` and leaves an anchor, so the chain verifies from the first event kept (`ops.tov` `auditRetention`). `audit.omit_ip_addresses` (L97p) stores no client address. Archive (L97p): `audit.archive` (the `object_storage` fields, `archive_interval`, `archive_grace_period`) copies each closed window as one JSONL object through `exports.tov` (`ops.tov` `auditArchive`); it never deletes rows, and its first run only sets a mark. L104: each closed window is written in Bifrost's layout (`audit_archive.tov` `planWindow`, under `object_storage.prefix`, default `bifrost`): `audit-logs/YYYY/MM/DD/<start>-<end>/part-NNNNN.jsonl` cut where a part reaches `archive_max_object_bytes` (default 128 MiB, kept within 1 MiB to 4 GiB; a line never splits), then `manifest.json` listing the parts, written last as the commit. An empty window still gets a manifest. `archive_compress` (`object_storage.compress`) gzips every object (`gzip.tov`: DEFLATE with fixed Huffman codes and greedy matches, checked with Python's gunzip). Each object is tried three times. The mark moves only after the manifest. L168: windows are fixed `archive_interval` windows aligned to the epoch (UTC), Bifrost's default 24h and 5 minute floor (`audit_archive.tov` `archiveIntervalMs`, `closedWindows`); each closed window is archived in order, one in flight at a time, its manifest committing it before the next starts, and the job wakes at least hourly. The grace period stays below the interval. A retried window can leave a part from the failed try that its manifest does not list, as in Bifrost, whose readers go by the manifest. Per-event verify, Bifrost's list filters and pages: see the (a3) /api/audit-logs row. L175: the access-profile audit lists, GET /api/governance/access-profiles/audit-logs and .../{profile_id}/audit-logs (also under /api/access-profiles) in Bifrost's AuditLogListResponse, read from the trail (`audit.tov` profileChange, profileAuditJson: create, update, delete, propagate, attach_role, detach_role, clone, activate, deactivate; action and user_id filters, limit and offset). previous_values is null, since the trail keeps the request and not the record before it. |

The audio token and per-second columns of the built-in table are merged from LiteLLM's public price file (L135, `scripts/gen_pricing.py --merge`), which Bifrost's datasheet descends from; the token rates stay the datasheet's, and a token rate that disagrees with the price file is reported by the merge, not applied. Only a live session bills per second (Bifrost's `computeLiveCost`, `livehooks.tov` `liveCost`). Audio requests are billed as Bifrost's `computeTranscriptionCost` and `computeSpeechCost` bill them (L144): a transcription by its audio and text tokens, else its prompt tokens, else its seconds at `input_cost_per_audio_per_second` (L169: read from the datasheet, `governance.pricing_overrides` and `config.pricing`), else at `input_cost_per_second`, as Bifrost's `tieredAudioInputPerSecondRate`, and its completion tokens at the output rate; speech by its input characters at `inputCharacter`, else at the text rate. `gateway.tov` `mediaAnswer` charges each 200 answer to its key's budgets and token limits, logs it and counts it in the metrics (a streamed transcription when it ends); a call governance refuses or the provider fails is logged as an error row with its status and message and charged nothing (L169, `mediaFailed`), and one that names no model or provider the gateway serves is answered before any provider, unlogged, as Bifrost answers it in its handler. The price table has the rows of OpenAI's `whisper-1`, `tts-1`, `tts-1-hd` and the live models `gpt-live-1`, `gpt-live-transcribe`, `gpt-realtime-whisper`, `gpt-realtime-translate` (added from the price file by `scripts/gen_pricing.py --merge`). A Gemini transcription is billed from its answer's `usageMetadata` as Bifrost bills it (L162). Not billed: speech output per second (Bifrost sets no seconds for speech either). The committed table has no `input_cost_per_audio_per_second` column: the current datasheet gives that rate to ten entries, none of them an audio model (embeddings and Gemma), so it bills nothing here; a synced datasheet carries it. Models outside it cost 0, and budgets skip them with a one-time warning (`gateway.tov`, `unpriced`). `client.refuse_unpriced_models` (L95) refuses budgeted requests to them with 403; off by default. |

L180: session tier retention (`complexity_router.session.enabled`; `gateway.tov` sessionTier, `complexity.tov` SessionTiers): a session (x-bf-session-id and the other session headers, scoped to its virtual key or credential and user, held by a digest) never steps down a tier within a day of its last request; a held COMPLEX and a tool continuation (the last message a tool result) take the session's tier unclassified, and the mechanism reads `session` when the session decided it. Kept per process, as Bifrost's in-memory KV store keeps it per node. semantic.fallback `llm` or `decision` asks that block when the semantic classifier names no tier (Raiju's heuristic answers after that, or with `none`). count_toward_budgets: a classifier's own call (semantic embeddings, the llm block) is kept off the caller's budgets unless its block sets it, by a per-process marker the call carries (`gateway.tov` classifierCall, NO_CHARGE_HEADER); the decision model's call is always charged, as in Bifrost. semantic.vector_store `vector_store` keeps the exemplars in the configured vector store under the generation's namespace and searches them there (`complexity.tov` storeExemplars, storeNearest; the status route reports storage_mode `vector_store`), in memory when no store is set or the store fails. e2e `l180_e2e`. | Adaptive load balancing (weights, held keys, incidents, API) | enterprise/adaptive-load-balancing.mdx | done | `health.tov`: weights. Held keys and incidents over the breakers (L97p): `GET /api/adaptive-routing/held-keys` (open `provider#key` circuits, held since, next check), `GET /api/adaptive-routing/incidents` (open circuits; resolved ones are not kept), `POST /api/adaptive-routing/conditions/clear` (release by target id, or all). L104: holds (`holds.tov`, `refusal.tov`). A provider's refusal is classed as Bifrost classes it (`core/failureclass.go`: credential, quota, model_access, model_gone, region_blocked, rate_limit; transient, caller_fault and unknown never hold a key). A credential or quota holds the whole key, the other classes one model. The wait is 30 s, doubled per failed check, capped per class (15 min, 5 min, 5 min, 30 min, 15 min, 2 min); a provider's Retry-After hint is used as given, never under 10 s; ladder waits vary by 10 percent. Holds exist only under adaptive load balancing (`health.adaptive`, Bifrost's load_balancer_config): with it off a refusal feeds the breaker alone and no key is held (L123). Key choice skips held keys (`router.tov` `pickHealthy`); when every key is held the request is sent anyway, to a held key this node owns the check for, else the one whose next check is soonest (`router.tov` `pickHealthy`). That request does not claim the check or climb the ladder: a refusal leaves the hold and its wait as they are, a served answer releases it (as Bifrost does for any answer), and only the owner's scheduled check is a check, so one check is sent cluster-wide. Across the cluster (L123): a hold is shared by every node. Holds travel in the cluster sync (`cluster.tov` `Counters.setHolds` and `takeHolds`; `holds.tov` `HoldBook.wire` and `merge`). Each target is a write-count register (ver, then the writer's id), so every node settles on the same state, and a release is a write that clears the key everywhere. Only the owner (the node that saw the refusal) sends the check when the wait ends; the others take over 60 s later, and only if the owner has not checked (the claim is gossiped, so they see it in flight). A hold's incident is derived from the same writes on every node, so the hold incidents agree across the cluster. When the wait ends, the next request is the check: a served check releases the hold, a refusal climbs the ladder, a 5xx or network error holds on the same rung. A key released within ten minutes for the same reason keeps its rung. Incidents: each hold and each circuit that failures open is kept in memory (500 at most). Routes: `GET /api/adaptive-routing/held-keys` (state, class, reason, wait, rung), `GET /api/adaptive-routing/incidents` (open), `GET /api/adaptive-routing/history/issues` (window, statuses, sort, limit and offset, matched_summary), `POST /api/adaptive-routing/conditions/clear` (keys as `provider#key` or `provider#key@model`). L165: the provider level (load_balancer_config's switches, see (c1) load_balancer_config): a provider whose keys are all held or broken is left for a healthy one when the request names no provider, and with reroute_failed_directions when it does; prune_failed_fallbacks and append_fallbacks_to_pinned act on the fallbacks. L180: a request pinned to a key (x-bf-api-key) is sent whatever its holds say, and its answer settles them as a check does (`holds.tov` claimPinned: a refusal climbs the ladder, a 5xx holds on the same rung, a served answer releases), as Bifrost documents. A google.rpc.RetryInfo detail in the error body (Gemini, Vertex) sets the wait over the retry headers (`refusal.tov` retryInfoMs, Bifrost's ApplyRetryInfo). Holds and incidents survive a restart: kept in `<config>.adaptive.json`, written every 30 seconds by the leader and at a graceful stop, restored at start (`holds.tov` snapshot, restore; `ops.tov` startAdaptiveStore); a circuit incident left open is resolved at the restore, since circuits start closed. Circuit incidents reach every node and worker through the replicated state (`clusterstate.tov`; `holds.tov` shareCircuit, peerCircuits), so the incidents and history routes list the cluster's. A takeover can send one check from each of two nodes; Bifrost lets each node send its own check, so this matches it. Releases travel with the next sync. e2e `l180_e2e`. |

| Projects | enterprise/projects.mdx | done | `store.tov` (`/api/governance/projects`), `governance.tov` (access_rule, accounting_mode), `config.tov` (`ProjectConfig`), x-bf-project-id and x-bf-project-name (L84). Members (L97p): `/api/governance/projects/{id}/members[/{member_id}]`, and a member's key is admitted to the project (`governance.tov` `projectAdmits`). split_policy `none` takes a member's own caps (a percent or an amount of a budget the project holds); `equal` gives each member an equal slice of every budget the project holds (`governance.tov` `addMemberCaps`; a slice is a window checked with the budget it divides). Rate-limit shares (L103): a member's `rate_limit_shares` (`token_share` and `request_share`, each a percent or an amount; split none) and, under split equal, a whole part of every token and request cap the project holds per member (`governance.tov` `addMemberCaps`, `rateShareOf`). A member's slice is a limit of its own, checked with the project's and debited with it. An equal split refuses a limit with fewer counts than the roster on save. L175: one member's usage, GET /api/governance/projects/{id}/members/{member_id}/usage (`ops.tov` memberUsage): each slice of the project's budgets and rate limits with its counters, and the member's requests in the project per provider and model since the slice's cycle began. L193: the redivision job. A management write that changes an equal-split project's roster or the budgets and rate limits it divides (a member add or removal, a project edit, a budget or rate-limit edit, a user deletion, which now also takes the user off every roster as Bifrost's cascade) queues a redivision job per project, reusing one not yet started (`ops.tov` `redivisionBefore`, `withRedivision`, `queueRedivision`); the project and member routes answer `redivision_job_id`, other writes `redivision_job_ids`. The job waits for the running config to hold the saved roster (the governance rebuild divides every cap), counts each member's slices, and reports Bifrost's `redivision_progress` events on /ws (redividing, then completed or error, with total, processed, succeeded and failed). A split none project queues none. e2e `l193_e2e`. |

| Auth: token exchange | mcp/auth; mcp_client_config.auth_type | done | L112b. `tokenexchange.tov` (the RFC 8693 form, the answer, the cache that keeps a token until 30 s before its lifetime ends, the token endpoint from `authorization_server_url` or the oidc discovery); `mcpapi.tov` (auth_type token_exchange with its block and validation, the client secret masked, `exchangeRouteAsync` for POST .../verify-exchange: the body's subject_token is exchanged, the server is dialed with the result and its tools counted; nothing is saved); `gateway.tov` `exchangeAuth` (per tool call: the caller's Authorization bearer, unless it is the virtual key, is exchanged and cached per client, caller and subject; a 401 from the server drops the cache entry). e2e: the exchange request, the masked view, the refusals and the no-save check. L133: the runtime connects a token_exchange client with the admin credential its verification kept: verify-exchange saves the exchanged token as `mcp.exchange_admin` (sealed by `store.tov`, never returned by the API; `mcp.tov` `adminTokenOf`, `McpSession.adminToken`, `connect`, and `follow`, which replaces a session when its credential changes), and a changed token_exchange block or a delete drops it. The tools are listed with that credential, as Bifrost's admin bootstrap token does, and each caller's call runs with the token exchanged for its own subject (`exchangeAuth`, cached per caller). L140: the admin credential is renewed as Bifrost's `refreshExchangeAdminToken` does: verify-exchange keeps the refresh token and expiry of the answer (sealed like the access token), and the gateway renews it with the refresh_token grant and the exchange's client credentials 5 minutes before it expires (halfway through a shorter life; `tokenexchange.tov` `renewAt`, `renewAdmin`; `gateway.tov` `renewAdminCredential`, `scheduleAdminRenewal`, at start before the clients connect and after each config change) and at once after a 401 from the server (`mcp.tov` `setAdminRenew`, `McpSession.exchange`). A rotated refresh token replaces the old one. A refusal for good (401, or 400 invalid_grant or unauthorized_client) or an expiry with no refresh token marks the credential `needs_reauth` with the reason (saved); GET /api/mcp and GET /api/mcp/clients then show `state: "needs_reauth"` and a `credential` block (status, status_reason, expires_at, last_refreshed_at, has_refresh_token; no token values), the client keeps its tools and each caller's calls, and only the listing stops until a new verification. Other failures retry after 30 s. The per-caller exchanged tokens keep their cache-until-expiry and are exchanged again once they run out, as Bifrost's are (no refresh token is kept for them). e2e: l140_e2e. L147: the renewal runs once across the workers of a node and the nodes of a cluster. Within a node the renewal takes a lease in the supervisor's share folder (`filelease.tov` `FileLease`: generations made with mkdir, a 60 s takeover; `gateway.tov` `renewAdminCredential` and `renewHeld`, under `adminLease`, which `main.tov` sets when RAIJU_WORKERS or -workers is above one, not on http1). A worker that finds it held waits up to 20 s for the holder's credential, read from the config file (`store.tov` `readStoredAdmin`), and renews only if that does not come, after a 401 too. Across nodes one node owns each client's renewal (`clusterstate.tov` `ownerOf`, rendezvous hashing over `ops.tov` `liveNodes`, `ownsAdmin`), and another node renews it only `ADMIN_TAKEOVER_MS` (60 s) after it falls due. The renewed credential, and the one verify-exchange keeps, go on the cluster state (`clusterstate.tov`, namespace `mcp.exchange_admin`, last writer wins by write time and writer, tombstones kept 10 min) with their tokens sealed (`store.tov` `sealAdminRow`). Other nodes open them with the same encryption_key and apply them to their config and live session (`main.tov`, `gateway.tov` `adoptRemoteAdmin`). Without an encryption_key the tokens travel as the config file holds them. The cluster transport is signed with cluster.secret, not encrypted. e2e: `l147_workers_e2e` (two workers, a rotating refresh token: one grant) and `l147_cluster_e2e` (two nodes). L167: re-checked the remaining items. A save never writes back an older credential than the file holds (L147, `store.tov` `keepNewerAdmin`, by last_refreshed_at), config.json writes are atomic (L158), and a deleted client's credential is tombstoned on the cluster state so the other nodes drop it; a token_exchange client and client.dual_credential_conflict_behavior error are refused together (see that row). L177: the MCP client configs are shared across the cluster (`mcpshare.tov`, wired in `main.tov`): a save that changes them puts each changed client on the cluster state (namespace `mcp.client`, sealed whole with the encryption key) and deletes the removed ones with a tombstone; each node applies the others' writes to the config as its file holds it and saves it, without sending it back; when the file holds it already (a sibling worker, a shared file) the config generation of that save (L185) rereads it, and with cluster.config_sync "pull" the whole config travels and the clients are not shared on their own. A client created, edited or deleted on one node is so on every node. During a membership change two nodes can both own a credential: a refusal of the refresh now waits up to ADMIN_WAIT_MS for the other node's rotated credential to arrive through the cluster state before it marks needs_reauth (`gateway.tov` `renewHeld`). The token endpoint of oidc.issuer is discovered at start and after a config change when a token_exchange client names no authorization_server_url (`main.tov` `discoverTokenEndpoint`), not on the first exchange. use_idp_credentials needing the oidc block is how Bifrost works too. e2e: l147_cluster_e2e (a client created on A, edited on B, deleted on A). P2. L188: the token endpoint (authorization_server_url or the issuer's discovered one) and the issuer's discovery document must be public addresses or named in `mcp.oauth_private_allowlist`, as Bifrost's OAuth client allows them, checked on the resolved addresses before each request and by the runtime when it connects; the exchange POST follows no redirect (`tokenexchange.tov` IdpEndpoints.allow, postForm). |
| Temporary MCP tokens (client.mcp_enable_temp_token_auth) | handlers/temptokens.go | done | L97o: with the flag on, a consent link carries a 15-minute `#t=` temp token (`mcpuser.tov` startUserFlow); `X-Bifrost-Temp-Token` opens that flow's GET detail and start routes (`ops.tov` gate, `mcpuser.tov` tempTokenAllows). L97r: the consent page sends the token for its own detail and start calls. L167: as Bifrost's scopes (temptokens.go): mcp_auth opens GET /api/oauth/per-user/flows/{id} and .../start of its flow, mcp_headers_auth GET and PUT /api/mcp/per-user-headers/flows/{id} of its submission; a token ends with its flow (the callback, a revoke, a replacing flow, a completed submission), as Bifrost deletes it by the resource, rather than after one route (Bifrost's page calls detail then start with the same token); user-mode and admin flows get none. Tokens are kept only as their SHA-256 in the flow state file, so a restart keeps them and every worker honours them. |

A tool call suspends the run and the program replays with the result cached, so each tool runs once. Limits: `mcp.code_mode_limits`, binding level `mcp.code_mode_binding_level` (server or tool). L166: on the Responses API the plain MCP tools are offered when no code mode client applies (`native.tov` responsesApi calls `engine.withMcpResponses`). As in Bifrost no agent runs on a stream: it is served live and the calls go to the caller; x-bf-mcp-agent: off skips it too. The agent's auto-execute check matches `server-*` patterns and `*` as code mode does (`agent.tov` `allowed`), so tools_to_auto_execute ["*"] works in the agent loop. A code mode client's tools stay callable through the execute route, as in Bifrost (executeToolInternal has no code mode check). e2e: l133_e2e, l177_e2e. |

### h2. Logs and traces

| Feature | Bifrost | Raiju status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Request log store (queryable) | plugins/logging; handlers/logging.go | done | `logstore.tov`: JSONL files and an in-memory index, with analytics (histograms, stats, rankings). The workers of a node append to one day file, and with several workers every log read goes to that file (`LogStore.shared`, `viewSince`, cached until the file grows), so any worker answers with every worker's requests; recost and delete rewrite the shared files. L182: a single process batches its writes (100 ms or 64 KB); workers still write row by row, so reads across them stay current; day files are cut into segments (`max_file_mb`) and reads take only the newest segments of today; `max_disk_mb` caps the folder. L179: a read whose window starts before the rows in hand (memory after a restart holds only today's rows) also gets the earlier days' rows from the day files (`logstore.tov` `viewSince`, `earlierRows`): up to max_memory_entries rows of the days before, newest first, cached until the files change (a new segment, a delete or a recost), for one process and for workers alike; a segment far over max_file_mb is not read on a query either. So the list, GET /api/logs/{id}, the sessions, stats and histograms reach yesterday's requests after a restart (e2e `l179_e2e`). L193: rows beyond memory are read by a paged scan (`logstore.tov` `scanRows`): a read whose window starts before the rows in hand reads the segments back from the newest that can hold it, today's included, with the cap applying to the rows of the window rather than the newest rows; a row's time is read from its line before it is parsed, and each segment read whole leaves its oldest and newest times in an index, so later scans skip segments outside their window; the list and stats pass their until, and GET /api/logs/{id} (and DELETE /api/logs) find a row anywhere in the files (`findById`). Cross-node reads (Bifrost's nodes share one database; Raiju's keep their own files): a node answering /api/logs (the list, one log, stats, sessions, histograms, rankings, dashboard, filterdata) or /api/mcp-logs asks each peer at once for its rows of the read's window over the signed POST /api/cluster/logs (`ops.tov` `logsRouteAll`, `readWindow`, `clusterLogs`; a few seconds at most, bodies only where the read shows them, a reveal mapping only for one row) and answers over its rows and theirs, merged by time with duplicates dropped; the caller's data scope and hidden request types apply on the asking node; a peer that does not answer is left out and reported once. `cluster.log_reads: false` (Raiju only) reads one node's rows. Not covered: broker mode (no peer addresses), and deletes and cost recalculation stay per node. e2e `l193_e2e`. |
| Live feed across the cluster (/ws resume, batches, relay) | not in Bifrost (its UI socket serves one node) | done (Raiju-only) | `feed.tov` (ring, cursors, queues, envelopes), `ops.tov` `UiHub`, `feedRelay`, `feedBroker`, `clusterFeed`, `broker.tov` `Relay.feedExchange` (broker mode), `server.tov` `wsUpgrade`. L119. Every event has `id` (producer:seq), `node` and `seq`; the producer is the node, or node/pid for a worker of a node with several. Events are relayed to the peers in batches (`cluster.feed.batch_ms`, default 100) or through the broker, and any node's /ws streams the merged feed. `?since=node:seq,...` replays what the client missed from the ring (`cluster.feed.ring_size`, `ring_seconds`), with a gap message for events the ring no longer holds; `?batch=1` sends arrays. A slow peer's queue drops its oldest events (`cluster.feed.queue_max`); a batch client over its buffer is closed with 1013. `cluster.feed.cluster_wide: false` keeps it on the node. Metrics: `raiju_feed_relayed_total`, `raiju_feed_dropped_total{reason}`, `raiju_feed_replayed_total`, `raiju_feed_gap_events_total`. Limits: a relay is a full mesh (a node's events go to its peers directly, and are not forwarded), so a node that is not a peer of a producer gets its events only through the broker; gap counts cover all events, not only those in the client's scope. |
| Content logging: global switch | client.disable_content_logging | done | `logs_store.content_logging` and `client.disable_content_logging` (`gateway.tov`, commit 12757de). |
| Content logging: per request | x-bf-disable-content-logging | done | `gateway.tov` (commit 12757de). |
| Log retention (days) | logs_store.retention_days; enterprise/log-exports.mdx | done | `logstore.tov`. |
| Log sinks: Datadog, Splunk HEC, Elasticsearch, generic HTTP | docs/features/observability/datadog.mdx, splunk.mdx | done | `exporters.tov` (SCOPE). |
| Log sinks: BigQuery, Kafka, Pub/Sub | docs/features/observability/bigquery.mdx, kafka.mdx, pubsub.mdx | done | `sink_gcp.tov` (BigQuery, Pub/Sub), `sink_kafka.tov`, wired by L89. |
| Log sink: Maxim | docs/features/observability/maxim.mdx; plugins/maxim | done | `sink_maxim.tov`, wired by L89. |
| Log exports to S3 and GCS | enterprise/log-exports.mdx | done | `exports.tov`. |
| Log exports: Parquet payloads, retention policy, redaction on export | enterprise/log-exports.mdx | done | Parquet (`sink_parquet.tov`) and export retention (L85) are done. Redaction (L97i): the content fields already hold the redacted text (`gateway.tov` chatOnce and the output guard); the raw upstream bodies (`raw_request`, `raw_response`) are now redacted too in redact mode (`gateway.tov` `redactRaw`), so exports carry them redacted. L97s: a raw response also gets the local output redact rules, and a streamed upstream body is not kept while local output guards apply (a match can span its events; the redacted answer is in the log's output). Not covered by e2e: a raw response that holds a secret the mock does not echo. L168: redaction_mode `logs_only` works for the local providers: the live request and answer stay raw, and what is stored (the log row's input, output and error, the raw payloads, log exports and retained content) gets Bifrost's numbered placeholders ([EMAIL-1], one vault per request) when the request is recorded (`gateway.tov` `record`, `logsOnlyText`). It covers the built-in detectors (`guardrails.logs_only`, set by the Bifrost mapping when every redact rule mapped to them asks for logs_only; mixed with runtime rules they redact live, with a warning) and a secrets provider's or a regex pattern's own rules (`splitLogsOnly` moves them out of the live rules). L179: the reveal mapping is kept: a logs_only row stores its placeholders' originals by phase ({"input": {"EMAIL-1": ...}, "output": {...}}, keys without brackets, as Bifrost's RedactionMapsByPhase) in the row's `redaction_mapping`, sealed with the config's encryption key when one is set and kept only when content logging is on for the request; GET /api/logs/{id} and GET /api/mcp-logs/{id} add it as `redaction_mapping`, with Cache-Control: no-store, for a caller holding Logs:Reveal (the admin, or a custom or edited built-in role with that permission; `ops.tov` `mayReveal`), and data access scope still applies; the list, the live feed, log exports and the rest of the reads never carry it (`logstore.tov` `publicEntry`). MCP tool logs: the stored error of a tool call gets the same logs_only placeholders, with its output-phase mapping for reveal (Raiju's MCP tool rows keep no arguments or results to redact). e2e `l179_e2e`. L193: logs_only on the HTTP providers: a Presidio or Azure AI Language PII provider with action redact and redaction_mode logs_only leaves the live request and answer raw, and its findings (entity type and original text) become the stored copy's numbered placeholders ([EMAIL_ADDRESS-1]) with the reveal mapping, in the same vault as the built-in detectors (`gateway.tov` `logsOnlyHttp`, `logsOnlyFound`). Per-request plumbing: the input check's findings wait for the row by request id, and a row whose answer such a provider still checks is held until the answer's findings are in (`heldRecords`, `releaseRecord`). The Bifrost mapping keeps logs_only for both providers (no warning). Streamed answers are not checked by HTTP output guards (as before). e2e `l193_e2e`. |

L195: `collector_url`, `metrics_endpoint` and the header values resolve `env.NAME` (`config.tov` otelEnvUrl gives an env URL the scheme and /v1/traces path a written one gets). |

Push deliveries are not agent operations and are not timed. The upstream transport attribute is not set. |

| SCIM and user provisioning | enterprise/user-provisioning.mdx | done | `scim.tov` and the `/scim/v2` routes in `server.tov` (L115): Users and Groups with filters, paging, PUT, PATCH (active false included) and delete; discovery documents; the SCIM error shape. Groups are teams. A pushed user takes a role from `roles` when it names a known one, else `viewer`. L181: claim mapping at sign-in when the identity provider is configured through scim_config (a provider set, and no role_map or default_role in the oidc section; `claimsync.tov`, `ops.tov` syncSignIn), as Bifrost's how-identity-sync-works describes it: the user is found by subject or email or created (named by its address), its role decided from the attribute mappings for roles (dot-path claims, values without regard to case, `*` and globs; several matches resolved by roleResolutionStrategy, the most permissions by default or list order) or with none from the roles claim (rolesField) naming a role; there is no default role: a sign-in that resolves none is refused, the role taken away when the claims say so (a mapped attribute with no matching value, a mapped role that does not exist) and kept when they say nothing; teams (the team mappings, `*` passing each value through, or the teamIdsField claim), business units (created when missing), projects (never created) and access profiles (by name) are resynced from their mappings, memberships an admin added kept apart from the ones claims gave (`synced`), the last value of each claim remembered so a token that leaves one out changes nothing. With SCIM provisioning on (a token set) and claimsSyncMode scim, a user SCIM has not pushed is refused ("Provisioning is managed by SCIM") and a pushed one signs in with the role SCIM gave. POST /api/scim/provisioning-token rotates the provisioning token (returned once; admin only). e2e `l181_scim_e2e`. L191 (`directory.tov`, `provision.tov`, `claimsync.tov`, `ops.tov` provisionRoute, refreshSession, configureDirectory): directory sync and bulk import read the identity provider's directory API (Okta's Users and Groups API with the apiToken, its Link-header pages; Microsoft Graph with client credentials, its users, group memberships and the app's roles assigned to a user or a group, its @odata.nextLink pages; Keycloak's Admin REST API with the client's service account, each user's groups by path and effective realm roles; Zitadel's user and grant search with a service account, machine users left out; Google's Directory API with a service account's signed assertion for adminEmail, its page tokens, users' primary organization, org unit, custom schemas and group addresses). Each user carries the attributes its sign-in token would (profile fields, groups, roles), so the same mapping rules decide it. POST /api/scim/provision/preview shows up to 50 users a filter selects (groups, roles, departments, ids) with what an import would do and the groups to filter by; POST /api/scim/provision/import creates or updates them; POST /api/scim/provision/sync (and the bulkSyncInterval schedule on the leader, GET /api/scim/provision/status) re-reads every user, creates or updates the active ones, removes identity-provider users the directory reports inactive and, after a complete listing, the ones it no longer lists once a lookup confirms them inactive or gone. Both answer 202 with an operation_id (GET /api/scim/provision/jobs/{operation_id}, the newest 50 kept) and are refused with 403 while SCIM provisioning is on. Removal takes the user's virtual keys and project memberships and ends its sessions; users an admin made, users with their own credentials and the last enabled admin are never removed. POST /api/scim/provision/reconcile re-decides users from the claims stored at their last sign-in, sync or SCIM push (what SCIM sent, in SCIM-only mode), all or user_ids, with keep_missing; .../reconcile/preview (a sample of 50 changes, who would lose every access profile, counts) and .../reconcile/preview/user show it without saving, and a reconcile sent with a preview's rules_hash under changed rules is refused with 409; manual users and users with no stored data are skipped. Session refresh: the refresh token stays on the server with the session, an expired session is refreshed silently before the management request and its fresh claims applied again (the access token's for Keycloak); a refresh the provider refuses ends the session and removes the user when the directory reports them inactive or gone (not in SCIM-only mode); a daily sweep on the leader refreshes sessions about to expire (skipped with SCIM provisioning on). SCIM pushes keep the attributes they send (flattened: core attributes, complex ones dotted, the enterprise and custom extensions by field name) and the SCIM groups a user is in, and with mapping rules every SCIM create, replace or patch re-decides the role, teams, business units, projects and access profiles from them through claimScimAttributes ("group" claims read the SCIM groups' names, "user" claims a renamed attribute, without regard to case); data that maps no role leaves the user with none; SCIM-only sign-ins re-apply the rules to what SCIM sent. A SCIM deactivation removes the user's keys, memberships and profiles and keeps its pushed profile (a reactivation restores what the rules give and its SCIM groups); a delete removes its keys too. A refusal that takes the role away still resyncs the memberships. A refused sign-in raises the "Sign-in refused" notice, at most once an hour per user and reason (`ops.tov` refused). The team member list and the new GET, POST /api/governance/business-units/{id}/users and DELETE .../users/{user_id} tell each member's source (manual, or synced from the identity provider: a mapping rule or a SCIM group), and a synced business-unit membership is not removed by hand (409). e2e `l191_e2e`. Open (DESIGN): ETags (Bifrost's docs do not describe them); the jobs live in memory and do not resume after a restart (Bifrost's durable job table; Raiju keeps no database, owner decision 5); the notice reaches every reader of /api/notifications (Raiju has no per-role notices). EXTERNAL: checks against a real identity provider. OIDC sign-in refuses a user that governance lists as disabled, matched by email or subject, and an open session of that user stops at its next request (L120); users governance does not list sign in as before. The identity-aware proxy and credentials refuse a disabled user too. |

L101 adds regex captures, the entropy filter, allowlists and stopwords, and generic-api-key is on. L172 (`bench/guard_secrets.tov`, 20 KB, best of 20 on a shared machine): keyword-dense code about 5 ms, every rule keyword about 15 ms, and a new worst case, text dense in the generic keywords with a value the generic rule reports at each (750 findings), down from about 52 to 45 ms: a pattern anchored with `^` tries only the start (`regex.tov` `Prog.anchored`), the visited table of the matcher is kept between runs and only the words a run set are cleared, allowlist patterns with required literals skip targets that hold none of them, and the generic rule's 1,500 stopwords are found by the secret's substrings in a set (`guard_regex.tov` `stopIn`); findings unchanged. L180: an allowlist pattern that is an alternation of many branches, each with a literal (the generic rule's match allowlist), gets an Aho-Corasick automaton over the branches' literals (`regex.tov` wideLiterals; `guard_regex.tov` LitMatcher, allowStarts): one pass finds every occurrence, and the pattern is tried only from the starts those occurrences can reach (each literal's byte bound and character set), with the answers of a full search (checked over generated targets). Findings-dense case on the same bench, best of 20: about 32 ms before, 17 ms after; keyword-dense code 3.6 ms, every rule keyword 10.4 ms; findings unchanged. |

| Guardrails: streaming output | enterprise/guardrails.mdx "Streaming Output Guardrails" | done | L97m: local output rules (regex and secrets providers, targets llm) check a chat stream as it accumulates (`guard_stream.tov`, `gateway.tov` StreamConverter.guarded). A block ends the stream with the error event (`guardrail_violation`, then [DONE]) and the upstream read stops. Text already sent is not recalled. L97s: the same guards on text completion streams (`text` frames) and Responses streams (passthrough and converted; a block ends them with an `event: error` event, and the summary events that repeat the answer carry the released text). On the Bun server, HTTP `/v1/responses` streams now go through the stream sink like chat (`server.tov` streamKind and routeStream), so the passthrough is live over HTTP; the http1 server streams them too (`front.tov` streamingKind and routeStream). Redact rules rewrite a stream as Bifrost's runtime redaction does: text is released up to the last whitespace at least 256 bytes behind the newest text, and held text goes out with the next event that carries no text or with the finish event. Streamed answers built whole (`Engine.guardWhole`) pass the guards before the cache and the log see them, so a replayed or logged stream is the redacted one; a block there is a 502, as for a whole answer. L107: the built-in guard (`guardrails.detect`, block or redact mode, `guard_stream.tov` `useBuiltin`) checks chat, text and Responses streams as the local rules do. A match that spans a release point is released whole or held: the release point moves back to the match's start (`guard_stream.tov` `cutPoint`, `spans`), and a PEM block is held from its BEGIN line until its END line closes. `guardrails.stream_replay_event_interval_ms` (Bifrost's rule key, carried through the Bifrost guardrails mapping as the largest output-rule value, capped at 1000) paces the events of a cached stream replayed to a streamed caller while a block-capable output guard is on (`gateway.tov` `replayPace`, `replayStream`). L131: a live stream under a block-capable output guard (a regex or secrets rule with a block action, or the built-in guard in block mode) is held as Bifrost does: no event goes to the client while the answer streams (`guard_stream.tov` `useHold`; redact rules still rewrite the held events), the guards check the accumulated text at growing steps and once more at the end, a block sends only the error event (nothing of the answer reaches the client, the upstream read stops at once), and a passing stream is replayed with `stream_replay_event_interval_ms` between events (`takeReplay`, `gateway.tov` `sendPaced`; the first event goes at once, 0 sends all at once). Chat, text completion and Responses streams, on both servers. Redact-only guards keep the windowed live path. Raiju bounds the hold, which Bifrost's docs do not: `guardrails.stream_hold_max_bytes` (default 8388608) and `stream_hold_max_ms` (default 300000), 0 lifts a bound; past a bound the stream is blocked with a `stream_hold` error, or with `stream_hold_on_limit: "release"` the held events are replayed and the stream continues live (later matches then cut it as before). The time cap is checked when a chunk arrives, so a silent upstream falls to stream_idle_timeout. Bifrost's docs and OSS source say nothing on keepalive comments or caps, so none are sent. A stream cut by an upstream failure drops its held events. Streams answered whole (non-live paths) are checked whole and not paced. Bifrost's semantic cache plugin has no replay interval; the key is a guardrail rule key. |
